# Alkali metal

Group of highly reactive chemical elements

Not to be confused with [Alkaline earth metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal").

| ---*Legend*primordial&#xA;element by radioactive decay                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

The **alkali metals** consist of the [chemical elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_element "Chemical element") [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium") (Li), [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium") (Na), [potassium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium "Potassium") (K),[\[note 1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-1) [rubidium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium") (Rb), [caesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium "Caesium") (Cs),[\[note 2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-5) and [francium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium "Francium") (Fr). Together with [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen "Hydrogen") they constitute [group 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_\(periodic_table\)#Group_names "Group (periodic table)"),[\[note 3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-group-numbering-7) which lies in the [s-block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-block "S-block") of the [periodic table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_table "Periodic table"). All alkali metals have their outermost electron in an [s-orbital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-orbital "S-orbital"): this shared electron configuration results in their having very similar characteristic properties.[\[note 4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-8) Indeed, the alkali metals provide the best example of [group trends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_trends "Periodic trends") in properties in the periodic table, with elements exhibiting well-characterised [homologous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homologous_series "Homologous series") behaviour.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) This family of elements is also known as the **lithium family** after its leading element.

The alkali metals are all shiny, [soft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardness "Hardness"), highly [reactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactivity_\(chemistry\) "Reactivity (chemistry)") [metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metals "Metals") at [standard temperature and pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_temperature_and_pressure "Standard temperature and pressure") and readily lose their [outermost electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valence_electron "Valence electron") to form [cations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cations "Cations") with [charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_charge "Electric charge") +1. They can all be cut easily with a knife due to their softness, exposing a shiny surface that tarnishes rapidly in air due to [oxidation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidation "Oxidation") by atmospheric moisture and [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen") (and in the case of lithium, [nitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen "Nitrogen")). Because of their high reactivity, they must be stored under oil to prevent reaction with air, and are found naturally only in [salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salts "Salts") and never as the free elements. Caesium, the fifth alkali metal, is the most reactive of all the metals. All the alkali metals react with water, with the heavier alkali metals reacting more vigorously than the lighter ones.

All of the discovered alkali metals occur in nature as their compounds: in order of [abundance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_of_the_chemical_elements "Abundance of the chemical elements"), sodium is the most abundant, followed by potassium, lithium, rubidium, caesium, and finally francium, which is very rare due to its extremely high [radioactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactivity "Radioactivity"); francium occurs only in minute [traces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trace_radioisotope "Trace radioisotope") in nature as an intermediate step in some obscure side branches of the natural [decay chains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decay_chain "Decay chain"). Experiments have been conducted to attempt the synthesis of [element 119](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Element_119 "Element 119"), which is likely to be the next member of the group; none were successful. However, ununennium may not be an alkali metal due to [relativistic effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_effects "Relativistic effects"), which are predicted to have a large influence on the chemical properties of [superheavy elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superheavy_element "Superheavy element"); even if it does turn out to be an alkali metal, it is predicted to have some differences in physical and chemical properties from its lighter homologues.

Most alkali metals have many different applications. One of the best-known applications of the pure elements is the use of rubidium and caesium in [atomic clocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_clock "Atomic clock"), of which caesium atomic clocks form the basis of the second. A common application of the compounds of sodium is the [sodium-vapour lamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium-vapour_lamp "Sodium-vapour lamp"), which emits light very efficiently. [Table salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_salt "Table salt"), or sodium chloride, has been used since antiquity. [Lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_\(medication\) "Lithium (medication)") finds use as a psychiatric medication and as an [anode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anode "Anode") in [lithium batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_batteries "Lithium batteries"). Sodium, potassium and possibly lithium are [essential elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_element "Essential element"), having major biological roles as [electrolytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrolytes "Electrolytes"), and although the other alkali metals are not essential, they also have various effects on the body, both beneficial and harmful.

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: History")]

[![A sample of petalite](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Petalite.jpg/220px-Petalite.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Petalite.jpg)

[Petalite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petalite "Petalite"), the lithium mineral from which lithium was first isolated

Sodium compounds have been known since ancient times; salt ([sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride")) has been an important commodity in human activities. While [potash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potash "Potash") has been used since ancient times, it was not understood for most of its history to be a fundamentally different substance from sodium mineral salts. [Georg Ernst Stahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Ernst_Stahl "Georg Ernst Stahl") obtained experimental evidence which led him to suggest the fundamental difference of sodium and potassium salts in 1702,[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-1702Suspect-10) and [Henri-Louis Duhamel du Monceau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri-Louis_Duhamel_du_Monceau "Henri-Louis Duhamel du Monceau") was able to prove this difference in 1736.[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11) The exact chemical composition of potassium and sodium compounds, and the status as chemical element of potassium and sodium, was not known then, and thus [Antoine Lavoisier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_Lavoisier "Antoine Lavoisier") did not include either alkali in his list of chemical elements in 1789.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-weeks-12)[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-disco-13)

Pure potassium was first isolated in 1807 in England by [Humphry Davy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humphry_Davy "Humphry Davy"), who derived it from [caustic potash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caustic_potash "Caustic potash") (KOH, potassium hydroxide) by the use of electrolysis of the molten salt with the newly invented [voltaic pile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaic_pile "Voltaic pile"). Previous attempts at electrolysis of the aqueous salt were unsuccessful due to potassium's extreme reactivity.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 68  Potassium was the first metal that was isolated by electrolysis.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Enghag2004-15) Later that same year, Davy reported extraction of sodium from the similar substance [caustic soda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caustic_soda "Caustic soda") (NaOH, lye) by a similar technique, demonstrating the elements, and thus the salts, to be different.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-weeks-12)[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-disco-13)[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Davy1807-16)[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-200disco-17)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/Johann_Wolfgang_D%C3%B6bereiner.jpg/170px-Johann_Wolfgang_D%C3%B6bereiner.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Johann_Wolfgang_D%C3%B6bereiner.jpg)

[Johann Wolfgang Döbereiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_D%C3%B6bereiner "Johann Wolfgang Döbereiner") was among the first to notice similarities between what are now known as the alkali metals.

[Petalite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petalite "Petalite") ([Li](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium")[Al](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium "Aluminium")[Si](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon "Silicon")4[O](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen")10) was discovered in 1800 by the Brazilian chemist [José Bonifácio de Andrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Bonif%C3%A1cio_de_Andrada "José Bonifácio de Andrada") in a mine on the island of [Utö, Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ut%C3%B6,_Sweden "Utö, Sweden").[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-mindat-18)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelementshistory-19)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-discovery-20) However, it was not until 1817 that [Johan August Arfwedson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johan_August_Arfwedson "Johan August Arfwedson"), then working in the laboratory of the chemist [Jöns Jacob Berzelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6ns_Jacob_Berzelius "Jöns Jacob Berzelius"), [detected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_the_chemical_elements "Discovery of the chemical elements") the presence of a new element while analysing petalite [ore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ore "Ore").[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-uwis-21)[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vanderkrogt-22) This new element was noted by him to form compounds similar to those of sodium and potassium, though its [carbonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_carbonate "Lithium carbonate") and [hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_hydroxide "Lithium hydroxide") were less [soluble in water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solubility "Solubility") and more [alkaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_\(chemistry\) "Base (chemistry)") than the other alkali metals.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-compounds-23) Berzelius gave the unknown material the name *lithion*/*lithina*, from the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek "Ancient Greek") word *λιθoς* (transliterated as *lithos*, meaning "stone"), to reflect its discovery in a solid mineral, as opposed to potassium, which had been discovered in plant ashes, and sodium, which was known partly for its high abundance in animal blood. He named the metal inside the material *lithium*.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-krebs-24)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelementshistory-19)[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vanderkrogt-22) Lithium, sodium, and potassium were part of the discovery of [periodicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_table "Periodic table"), as they are among a series of triads of elements in the same [group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_\(periodic_table\) "Group (periodic table)") that were noted by [Johann Wolfgang Döbereiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_D%C3%B6bereiner "Johann Wolfgang Döbereiner") in 1850 as having similar properties.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-meta-synthesis2-25)

[![A sample of lepidolite](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Lepidolite-76774.jpg/170px-Lepidolite-76774.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lepidolite-76774.jpg)

[Lepidolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite"), the rubidium mineral from which rubidium was first isolated

Rubidium and caesium were the first elements to be discovered using the [spectroscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectroscope "Spectroscope"), invented in 1859 by [Robert Bunsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bunsen "Robert Bunsen") and [Gustav Kirchhoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Kirchhoff "Gustav Kirchhoff").[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-caesium-26) The next year, they discovered caesium in the [mineral water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_water "Mineral water") from [Bad Dürkheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_D%C3%BCrkheim "Bad Dürkheim"), Germany. Their discovery of rubidium came the following year in [Heidelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heidelberg "Heidelberg"), Germany, finding it in the mineral [lepidolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite").[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-BuKi1861-27) The names of rubidium and caesium come from the most prominent lines in their [emission spectra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_spectra "Emission spectra"): a bright red line for rubidium (from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin "Latin") word *rubidus*, meaning dark red or bright red), and a sky-blue line for caesium (derived from the Latin word *caesius*, meaning sky-blue).[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Weeks-28)[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29)

Around 1865 [John Newlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Alexander_Reina_Newlands "John Alexander Reina Newlands") produced a series of papers where he listed the elements in order of increasing atomic weight and similar physical and chemical properties that recurred at intervals of eight; he likened such periodicity to the [octaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octave "Octave") of music, where notes an octave apart have similar musical functions.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31) His version put all the alkali metals then known (lithium to caesium), as well as copper, silver, and [thallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thallium "Thallium") (which show the +1 oxidation state characteristic of the alkali metals), together into a group. His table placed hydrogen with the [halogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halogen "Halogen").[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-meta-synthesis2-25)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Mendelejevs_periodiska_system_1871.png/390px-Mendelejevs_periodiska_system_1871.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mendelejevs_periodiska_system_1871.png)

[Dmitri Mendeleev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitri_Mendeleev "Dmitri Mendeleev")'s periodic system proposed in 1871 showing hydrogen and the alkali metals as part of his group I, along with copper, silver, and gold

After 1869, [Dmitri Mendeleev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitri_Mendeleev "Dmitri Mendeleev") proposed his periodic table placing lithium at the top of a group with sodium, potassium, rubidium, caesium, and thallium.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) Two years later, Mendeleev revised his table, placing hydrogen in group 1 above lithium, and also moving thallium to the [boron group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron_group "Boron group"). In this 1871 version, copper, silver, and gold were placed twice, once as part of [group IB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_11_element "Group 11 element"), and once as part of a "group VIII" encompassing today's groups [8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_8_element "Group 8 element") to 11.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jensen-33)[\[note 5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-34) After the introduction of the 18-column table, the group IB elements were moved to their current position in the [d-block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D-block "D-block"), while alkali metals were left in *group IA*. Later the group's name was changed to *group 1* in 1988.[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fluck-6) The [trivial name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trivial_name "Trivial name") "alkali metals" comes from the fact that the hydroxides of the group 1 elements are all strong [alkalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali "Alkali") when dissolved in water.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)

There were at least four erroneous and incomplete discoveries[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fontani-35)[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vanderkrogt-Fr-36)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) before [Marguerite Perey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marguerite_Perey "Marguerite Perey") of the [Curie Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curie_Institute_\(Paris\) "Curie Institute (Paris)") in Paris, France discovered francium in 1939 by purifying a sample of [actinium-227](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actinium-227 "Actinium-227"), which had been reported to have a decay energy of 220 [keV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KeV "KeV"). However, Perey noticed decay particles with an energy level below 80 keV. Perey thought this decay activity might have been caused by a previously unidentified decay product, one that was separated during purification, but emerged again out of the pure [actinium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actinium "Actinium")-227. Various tests eliminated the possibility of the unknown element being [thorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thorium "Thorium"), [radium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radium "Radium"), lead, [bismuth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismuth "Bismuth"), or [thallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thallium "Thallium"). The new product exhibited chemical properties of an alkali metal (such as coprecipitating with caesium salts), which led Perey to believe that it was element 87, caused by the [alpha decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_decay "Alpha decay") of actinium-227.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemeducator-39) Perey then attempted to determine the proportion of [beta decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_decay "Beta decay") to alpha decay in actinium-227. Her first test put the alpha branching at 0.6%, a figure that she later revised to 1%.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-mcgraw-40)

- 227\
  89Acα (1.38%)→21.77 y **223\
  87Fr**β−→22 min 223\
  88Raα→11.4 d219\
  86Rn

The next element below francium ([eka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendeleev%27s_predicted_elements "Mendeleev's predicted elements")-francium) in the periodic table would be [ununennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ununennium "Ununennium") (Uue), element 119.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  The synthesis of ununennium was first attempted in 1985 by bombarding a target of [einsteinium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einsteinium "Einsteinium")-254 with [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium")-48 ions at the superHILAC accelerator at the [Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Berkeley_National_Laboratory "Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory") in Berkeley, California. No atoms were identified, leading to a limiting yield of 300 [nb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barn_\(unit\) "Barn (unit)").[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-link-42)[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vanderkrogt-uue-43)

- [254\
  99Es](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einsteinium-254 "Einsteinium-254")+ [48\
  20Ca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium-48 "Calcium-48")→ 302\
  119Uue\* → *no atoms*[\[note 6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44)

It is highly unlikely[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-link-42) that this reaction will be able to create any atoms of ununennium in the near future, given the extremely difficult task of making sufficient amounts of einsteinium-254, which is favoured for production of [ultraheavy elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superheavy_element "Superheavy element") because of its large mass, relatively long half-life of 270 days, and availability in significant amounts of several micrograms,[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45) to make a large enough target to increase the sensitivity of the experiment to the required level; einsteinium has not been found in nature and has only been produced in laboratories, and in quantities smaller than those needed for effective synthesis of superheavy elements. However, given that ununennium is only the first [period 8 element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_8_element "Period 8 element") on the [extended periodic table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_periodic_table "Extended periodic table"), it may well be discovered in the near future through other reactions, and indeed an attempt to synthesise it is currently ongoing in Japan.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Enyo-46) Currently, none of the period 8 elements has been discovered yet, and it is also possible, due to [drip instabilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleon_drip_line "Nucleon drip line"), that only the lower period 8 elements, up to around element 128, are physically possible.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-EB-47)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-emsley-48) No attempts at synthesis have been made for any heavier alkali metals: due to their extremely high atomic number, they would require new, more powerful methods and technology to make.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1737–1739 

## Occurrence

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Occurrence")]

### In the Solar System

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: In the Solar System")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/SolarSystemAbundances.svg/550px-SolarSystemAbundances.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SolarSystemAbundances.svg)

Estimated abundances of the chemical elements in the Solar system. Hydrogen and helium are most common, from the [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang "Big Bang"). The next three elements (lithium, [beryllium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryllium "Beryllium"), and [boron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron "Boron")) are rare because they are poorly synthesised in the Big Bang and also in stars. The two general trends in the remaining stellar-produced elements are: (1) an alternation of abundance in elements as they have even or odd atomic numbers, and (2) a general decrease in abundance, as elements become heavier. Iron is especially common because it represents the minimum-energy nuclide that can be made by fusion of helium in supernovae.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-lodders-49)

The [Oddo–Harkins rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oddo%E2%80%93Harkins_rule "Oddo–Harkins rule") holds that elements with even atomic numbers are more common that those with odd atomic numbers, with the exception of hydrogen. This rule argues that elements with odd atomic numbers have one unpaired proton and are more likely to capture another, thus increasing their atomic number. In elements with even atomic numbers, protons are paired, with each member of the pair offsetting the spin of the other, enhancing stability.[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-oddo-50)[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-harkins-51)[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-north-52) All the alkali metals have odd atomic numbers and they are not as common as the elements with even atomic numbers adjacent to them (the [noble gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_gas "Noble gas") and the [alkaline earth metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal")) in the Solar System. The heavier alkali metals are also less abundant than the lighter ones as the alkali metals from rubidium onward can only be synthesised in [supernovae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova "Supernova") and not in [stellar nucleosynthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_nucleosynthesis "Stellar nucleosynthesis"). Lithium is also much less abundant than sodium and potassium as it is poorly synthesised in both [Big Bang nucleosynthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang_nucleosynthesis "Big Bang nucleosynthesis") and in stars: the Big Bang could only produce trace quantities of lithium, [beryllium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryllium "Beryllium") and [boron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron "Boron") due to the absence of a stable nucleus with 5 or 8 [nucleons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleon "Nucleon"), and stellar nucleosynthesis could only pass this bottleneck by the [triple-alpha process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple-alpha_process "Triple-alpha process"), fusing three helium nuclei to form [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon "Carbon"), and skipping over those three elements.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-lodders-49)

### On Earth

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: On Earth")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Spodumene-usa59abg.jpg/170px-Spodumene-usa59abg.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Spodumene-usa59abg.jpg)

[Spodumene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spodumene "Spodumene"), an important lithium mineral

The Earth formed from the same cloud of matter that formed the Sun, but the planets acquired different compositions during the [formation and evolution of the solar system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_and_evolution_of_the_solar_system "Formation and evolution of the solar system"). In turn, the [natural history of the Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Earth "History of Earth") caused parts of this planet to have differing concentrations of the elements. The mass of the Earth is approximately 5.98×1024 kg. It is composed mostly of iron (32.1%), [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen") (30.1%), [silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon "Silicon") (15.1%), [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium "Magnesium") (13.9%), [sulfur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur "Sulfur") (2.9%), [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel "Nickel") (1.8%), [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium") (1.5%), and aluminium (1.4%); with the remaining 1.2% consisting of trace amounts of other elements. Due to [planetary differentiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_differentiation "Planetary differentiation"), the core region is believed to be primarily composed of iron (88.8%), with smaller amounts of nickel (5.8%), sulfur (4.5%), and less than 1% trace elements.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pnas71_12_6973-53)

The alkali metals, due to their high reactivity, do not occur naturally in pure form in nature. They are [lithophiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldschmidt_classification "Goldschmidt classification") and therefore remain close to the Earth's surface because they combine readily with [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen") and so associate strongly with [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silica "Silica"), forming relatively low-density minerals that do not sink down into the Earth's core. Potassium, rubidium and caesium are also [incompatible elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incompatible_element "Incompatible element") due to their large [ionic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_radii "Ionic radii").[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-albarede-54)

Sodium and potassium are very abundant on Earth, both being among the ten [most common elements in Earth's crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_of_elements_in_Earth%27s_crust "Abundance of elements in Earth's crust");[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-occurrence-55)[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-IsraelScience\&Technology-56) sodium makes up approximately 2.6% of the Earth's crust measured by weight, making it the [sixth most abundant element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_of_the_chemical_elements "Abundance of the chemical elements") overall[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible86th-57) and the most abundant alkali metal. Potassium makes up approximately 1.5% of the Earth's crust and is the seventh most abundant element.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible86th-57) Sodium is found in many different minerals, of which the most common is ordinary salt (sodium chloride), which occurs in vast quantities dissolved in seawater. Other solid deposits include [halite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halite "Halite"), [amphibole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibole "Amphibole"), [cryolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryolite "Cryolite"), [nitratine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitratine "Nitratine"), and [zeolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeolite "Zeolite").[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible86th-57) Many of these solid deposits occur as a result of ancient seas evaporating, which still occurs now in places such as [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah "Utah")'s [Great Salt Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Salt_Lake "Great Salt Lake") and the [Dead Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea "Dead Sea").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 69  Despite their near-equal abundance in Earth's crust, sodium is far more common than potassium in the ocean, both because potassium's larger size makes its salts less soluble, and because potassium is bound by silicates in soil and what potassium leaches is absorbed far more readily by plant life than sodium.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 69 

Despite its chemical similarity, lithium typically does not occur together with sodium or potassium due to its smaller size.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 69  Due to its relatively low reactivity, it can be found in seawater in large amounts; it is estimated that lithium concentration in seawater is approximately 0.14 to 0.25 parts per million (ppm)[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58)[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-enc-59) or 25 [micromolar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micromolar "Micromolar").[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60) Its diagonal relationship with magnesium often allows it to replace magnesium in [ferromagnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ferromagnesium\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Ferromagnesium (page does not exist)") minerals, where its crustal concentration is about 18 [ppm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parts_per_million "Parts per million"), comparable to that of [gallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallium "Gallium") and [niobium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niobium "Niobium"). Commercially, the most important lithium mineral is [spodumene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spodumene "Spodumene"), which occurs in large deposits worldwide.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 69 

Rubidium is approximately as abundant as [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc "Zinc") and more abundant than copper. It occurs naturally in the minerals [leucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leucite "Leucite"), [pollucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollucite "Pollucite"), [carnallite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnallite "Carnallite"), [zinnwaldite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinnwaldite "Zinnwaldite"), and [lepidolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite"),[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-61) although none of these contain only rubidium and no other alkali metals.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 70  Caesium is more abundant than some commonly known elements, such as [antimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimony "Antimony"), [cadmium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadmium "Cadmium"), [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin "Tin"), and [tungsten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tungsten "Tungsten"), but is much less abundant than rubidium.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pubs.usgs-62)

[Francium-223](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium-223 "Francium-223"), the only naturally occurring isotope of francium,[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomicweights2007-63)[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomicweights2009-64) is the [product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decay_product "Decay product") of the [alpha decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_decay "Alpha decay") of actinium-227 and can be found in trace amounts in [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") minerals.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CRC2006-65) In a given sample of uranium, there is estimated to be only one francium atom for every 1018 uranium atoms.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nbb-66)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-elemental-67) It has been calculated that there are at most 30 grams of francium in the [earth's crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crust_\(geology\) "Crust (geology)") at any time, due to its extremely short [half-life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-life "Half-life") of 22 minutes.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Winter-68)[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-itselemental-69)

## Properties

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Properties")]

### Physical and chemical

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Physical and chemical")]

The physical and chemical properties of the alkali metals can be readily explained by their having an ns1 valence [electron configuration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_configuration "Electron configuration"), which results in weak [metallic bonding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_bonding "Metallic bonding"). Hence, all the alkali metals are soft and have low [densities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Densities "Densities"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) [melting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_point "Melting point")[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and [boiling points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boiling_point "Boiling point"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) as well as [heats of sublimation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_of_sublimation "Heat of sublimation"), [vaporisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_of_vaporization "Heat of vaporization"), and [dissociation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissociation_\(chemistry\) "Dissociation (chemistry)").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74  They all crystallise in the [body-centered cubic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body-centered_cubic "Body-centered cubic") crystal structure,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 73  and have distinctive [flame colours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_test "Flame test") because their outer s electron is very easily excited.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  Indeed, these flame test colours are the most common way of identifying them since all their salts with common ions are soluble.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  The ns1 configuration also results in the alkali metals having very large [atomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radius "Atomic radius") and [ionic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_radii "Ionic radii"), as well as very high [thermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_conductivity "Thermal conductivity") and [electrical conductivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_conductivity "Electrical conductivity").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  Their chemistry is dominated by the loss of their lone valence electron in the outermost s-orbital to form the +1 oxidation state, due to the ease of ionising this electron and the very high second ionisation energy.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76  Most of the chemistry has been observed only for the first five members of the group. The chemistry of francium is not well established due to its extreme [radioactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactivity "Radioactivity");[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) thus, the presentation of its properties here is limited. What little is known about francium shows that it is very close in behaviour to caesium, as expected. The physical properties of francium are even sketchier because the bulk element has never been observed; hence any data that may be found in the literature are certainly speculative extrapolations.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible84th-70)

The alkali metals are more similar to each other than the elements in any other [group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_\(periodic_table\) "Group (periodic table)") are to each other.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) Indeed, the similarity is so great that it is quite difficult to separate potassium, rubidium, and caesium, due to their similar [ionic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_radii "Ionic radii"); lithium and sodium are more distinct. For instance, when moving down the table, all known alkali metals show increasing [atomic radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radius "Atomic radius"),[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) decreasing [electronegativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronegativity "Electronegativity"),[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) increasing [reactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactivity_\(chemistry\) "Reactivity (chemistry)"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and decreasing melting and boiling points[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) as well as heats of fusion and vaporisation.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  In general, their [densities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Densities "Densities") increase when moving down the table, with the exception that potassium is less dense than sodium.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) One of the very few properties of the alkali metals that does not display a very smooth trend is their [reduction potentials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reduction_potential "Reduction potential"): lithium's value is anomalous, being more negative than the others.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  This is because the Li+ ion has a very high [hydration energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydration_energy "Hydration energy") in the gas phase: though the lithium ion disrupts the structure of water significantly, causing a higher change in entropy, this high hydration energy is enough to make the reduction potentials indicate it as being the most electropositive alkali metal, despite the difficulty of ionising it in the gas phase.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75 

The stable alkali metals are all silver-coloured metals except for caesium, which has a pale golden tint:[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-theodoregray-caesium-82) it is one of only three metals that are clearly coloured (the other two being copper and gold).[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74  Additionally, the heavy [alkaline earth metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal") [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium"), [strontium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strontium "Strontium"), and [barium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barium "Barium"), as well as the divalent [lanthanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanthanide "Lanthanide") [europium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europium "Europium") and [ytterbium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ytterbium "Ytterbium"), are pale yellow, though the colour is much less prominent than it is for caesium.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74  Their lustre tarnishes rapidly in air due to oxidation.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Potassium_water_20.theora.ogv/220px--Potassium_water_20.theora.ogv.jpg)](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/4/43/Potassium_water_20.theora.ogv/Potassium_water_20.theora.ogv.480p.vp9.webm)

Potassium reacts violently with water at room temperature

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Cesium_water.theora.ogv/220px--Cesium_water.theora.ogv.jpg)](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/9b/Cesium_water.theora.ogv/Cesium_water.theora.ogv.480p.vp9.webm)

Caesium reacts explosively with water even at low temperatures

All the alkali metals are highly reactive and are never found in elemental forms in nature.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-krebs-24) Because of this, they are usually stored in [mineral oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_oil "Mineral oil") or [kerosene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerosene "Kerosene") (paraffin oil).[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-OU-83) They react aggressively with the [halogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halogen "Halogen") to form the [alkali metal halides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_metal_halide "Alkali metal halide"), which are white [ionic crystalline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_crystal "Ionic crystal") compounds that are all [soluble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soluble "Soluble") in water except [lithium fluoride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_fluoride "Lithium fluoride") (LiF).[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) The alkali metals also react with water to form strongly [alkaline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali "Alkali") [hydroxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxide "Hydroxide") and thus should be handled with great care. The heavier alkali metals react more vigorously than the lighter ones; for example, when dropped into water, caesium produces a larger explosion than potassium if the same number of moles of each metal is used.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalibangs-84)[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pubs.usgs-62) The alkali metals have the lowest first [ionisation energies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionisation_energies "Ionisation energies") in their respective periods of the [periodic table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_table "Periodic table")[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible84th-70) because of their low [effective nuclear charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_nuclear_charge "Effective nuclear charge")[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and the ability to attain a [noble gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_gas "Noble gas") configuration by losing just one [electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron "Electron").[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) Not only do the alkali metals react with water, but also with proton donors like [alcohols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_\(chemistry\) "Alcohol (chemistry)") and [phenols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenols "Phenols"), gaseous [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia"), and [alkynes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkyne "Alkyne"), the last demonstrating the phenomenal degree of their reactivity. Their great power as reducing agents makes them very useful in liberating other metals from their oxides or halides.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76 

The second ionisation energy of all of the alkali metals is very high[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RubberBible84th-70) as it is in a full shell that is also closer to the nucleus;[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) thus, they almost always lose a single electron, forming cations.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  The [alkalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkalide "Alkalide") are an exception: they are unstable compounds which contain alkali metals in a −1 oxidation state, which is very unusual as before the discovery of the alkalides, the alkali metals were not expected to be able to form [anions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anion "Anion") and were thought to be able to appear in [salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salts "Salts") only as cations. The alkalide anions have filled [s-subshells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-orbital "S-orbital"), which gives them enough stability to exist. All the stable alkali metals except lithium are known to be able to form alkalides,[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87) and the alkalides have much theoretical interest due to their unusual [stoichiometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoichiometry "Stoichiometry") and low [ionisation potentials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionization_potential "Ionization potential"). Alkalides are chemically similar to the [electrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electride "Electride"), which are salts with trapped [electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron "Electron") acting as anions.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Redko-88) A particularly striking example of an alkalide is "inverse [sodium hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_hydride "Sodium hydride")", H+Na− (both ions being [complexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordination_complex "Coordination complex")), as opposed to the usual sodium hydride, Na+H−:[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HNa-89) it is unstable in isolation, due to its high energy resulting from the displacement of two electrons from hydrogen to sodium, although several derivatives are predicted to be [metastable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metastable "Metastable") or stable.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HNa-89)[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HNa-theory-90)

In aqueous solution, the alkali metal ions form [aqua ions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metal_ions_in_aqueous_solution "Metal ions in aqueous solution") of the formula \[M(H2O)*n*]+, where *n* is the solvation number. Their [coordination numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordination_number "Coordination number") and shapes agree well with those expected from their ionic radii. In aqueous solution the water molecules directly attached to the metal ion are said to belong to the [first coordination sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_coordination_sphere "First coordination sphere"), also known as the first, or primary, solvation shell. The bond between a water molecule and the metal ion is a [dative covalent bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dative_covalent_bond "Dative covalent bond"), with the oxygen atom donating both electrons to the bond. Each coordinated water molecule may be attached by [hydrogen bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_bond "Hydrogen bond") to other water molecules. The latter are said to reside in the second coordination sphere. However, for the alkali metal cations, the second coordination sphere is not well-defined as the +1 charge on the cation is not high enough to [polarise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarizability "Polarizability") the water molecules in the primary solvation shell enough for them to form strong hydrogen bonds with those in the second coordination sphere, producing a more stable entity.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91)[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Richens-92): 25  The solvation number for Li+ has been experimentally determined to be 4, forming the [tetrahedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrahedral "Tetrahedral") \[Li(H2O)4]+: while solvation numbers of 3 to 6 have been found for lithium aqua ions, solvation numbers less than 4 may be the result of the formation of contact [ion pairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_pair "Ion pair"), and the higher solvation numbers may be interpreted in terms of water molecules that approach \[Li(H2O)4]+ through a face of the tetrahedron, though molecular dynamic simulations may indicate the existence of an [octahedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octahedral "Octahedral") hexaaqua ion. There are also probably six water molecules in the primary solvation sphere of the sodium ion, forming the octahedral \[Na(H2O)6]+ ion.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Richens-92): 126–127  While it was previously thought that the heavier alkali metals also formed octahedral hexaaqua ions, it has since been found that potassium and rubidium probably form the \[K(H2O)8]+ and \[Rb(H2O)8]+ ions, which have the [square antiprismatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_antiprism "Square antiprism") structure, and that caesium forms the 12-coordinate \[Cs(H2O)12]+ ion.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93)

#### Lithium

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Lithium")]

The chemistry of lithium shows several differences from that of the rest of the group as the small Li+ cation [polarises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_polarity "Chemical polarity") [anions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anion "Anion") and gives its compounds a more [covalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent "Covalent") character.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) Lithium and [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium "Magnesium") have a [diagonal relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diagonal_relationship "Diagonal relationship") due to their similar atomic radii,[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) so that they show some similarities. For example, lithium forms a stable [nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitride "Nitride"), a property common among all the [alkaline earth metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal") (magnesium's group) but unique among the alkali metals.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalireact-94) In addition, among their respective groups, only lithium and magnesium form [organometallic compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organometallic_compound "Organometallic compound") with significant covalent character (e.g. Li[Me](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methyl_group "Methyl group") and MgMe2).[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Shriver\&Atkins-95)

Lithium fluoride is the only alkali metal halide that is poorly soluble in water,[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and [lithium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_hydroxide "Lithium hydroxide") is the only alkali metal hydroxide that is not [deliquescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deliquescent "Deliquescent").[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) Conversely, [lithium perchlorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_perchlorate "Lithium perchlorate") and other lithium salts with large anions that cannot be polarised are much more stable than the analogous compounds of the other alkali metals, probably because Li+ has a high [solvation energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solvation_energy "Solvation energy").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76  This effect also means that most simple lithium salts are commonly encountered in hydrated form, because the anhydrous forms are extremely [hygroscopic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hygroscopic "Hygroscopic"): this allows salts like [lithium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_chloride "Lithium chloride") and [lithium bromide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_bromide "Lithium bromide") to be used in [dehumidifiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehumidifier "Dehumidifier") and [air-conditioners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air-conditioner "Air-conditioner").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76 

#### Francium

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Francium")]

Francium is also predicted to show some differences due to its high [atomic weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_weight "Atomic weight"), causing its electrons to travel at considerable fractions of the speed of light and thus making [relativistic effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_effects "Relativistic effects") more prominent. In contrast to the trend of decreasing [electronegativities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronegativities "Electronegativities") and [ionisation energies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionisation_energies "Ionisation energies") of the alkali metals, francium's electronegativity and ionisation energy are predicted to be higher than caesium's due to the relativistic stabilisation of the 7s electrons; also, its [atomic radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radius "Atomic radius") is expected to be abnormally low. Thus, contrary to expectation, caesium is the most reactive of the alkali metals, not francium.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-andreev-76)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729 [\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Thayer-96) All known physical properties of francium also deviate from the clear trends going from lithium to caesium, such as the first ionisation energy, electron affinity, and anion polarisability, though due to the paucity of known data about francium many sources give extrapolated values, ignoring that relativistic effects make the trend from lithium to caesium become inapplicable at francium.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Thayer-96) Some of the few properties of francium that have been predicted taking relativity into account are the electron affinity (47.2 kJ/mol)[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Landaualkalis-97) and the enthalpy of dissociation of the Fr2 molecule (42.1 kJ/mol).[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Liddle-98) The CsFr molecule is polarised as Cs+Fr−, showing that the 7s subshell of francium is much more strongly affected by relativistic effects than the 6s subshell of caesium.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Thayer-96) Additionally, francium superoxide (FrO2) is expected to have significant covalent character, unlike the other alkali metal superoxides, because of bonding contributions from the 6p electrons of francium.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Thayer-96)

### Nuclear

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Nuclear")]

| Z                                                                                                                                                  | Alkali metal                                                     | [Stable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stable_isotope "Stable isotope")          | *[Decays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primordial_element "Primordial element")* | *unstable: italics*odd–odd isotopes coloured pink                                                                                  |        |       |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------ | ----- |
| 3                                                                                                                                                  | [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium")       | [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_lithium "Isotopes of lithium")     | —                                                                                 | 7Li                                                                                                                                | 6Li    |       |
| 11                                                                                                                                                 | [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium")          | [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_sodium "Isotopes of sodium")       | —                                                                                 | 23Na                                                                                                                               |        |       |
| 19                                                                                                                                                 | [potassium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium "Potassium") | [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_potassium "Isotopes of potassium") | 1                                                                                 | 39K                                                                                                                                | 41K    | *40K* |
| 37                                                                                                                                                 | [rubidium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium")    | [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_rubidium "Isotopes of rubidium")   | 1                                                                                 | 85Rb                                                                                                                               | *87Rb* |       |
| 55                                                                                                                                                 | [caesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium "Caesium")       | [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_caesium "Isotopes of caesium")     | —                                                                                 | 133Cs                                                                                                                              |        |       |
| 87                                                                                                                                                 | [francium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium "Francium")    | [—](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_francium "Isotopes of francium")   | —                                                                                 | *No primordial isotopes* (*223Fr*is a [radiogenic nuclide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiogenic_nuclide "Radiogenic nuclide")) |        |       |
| Radioactive: 40K, [t1/2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-life "Half-life") 1.25 × 109 years; 87Rb, t1/2 4.9 × 1010 years; 223Fr, t1/2 22.0 min. |                                                                  |                                                                                  |                                                                                   |                                                                                                                                    |        |       |

All the alkali metals have odd atomic numbers; hence, their isotopes must be either [odd–odd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odd%E2%80%93odd_nuclei "Odd–odd nuclei") (both proton and [neutron number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_number "Neutron number") are odd) or [odd–even](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odd%E2%80%93even_nuclei "Odd–even nuclei") ([proton number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proton_number "Proton number") is odd, but neutron number is even). Odd–odd nuclei have even [mass numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_number "Mass number"), whereas odd–even nuclei have odd mass numbers. Odd–odd [primordial nuclides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primordial_nuclide "Primordial nuclide") are rare because most odd–odd nuclei are highly unstable with respect to [beta decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_decay "Beta decay"), because the decay products are even–even, and are therefore more strongly bound, due to [nuclear pairing effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-empirical_mass_formula#Pairing_term "Semi-empirical mass formula").[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Lide02-99)

Due to the great rarity of odd–odd nuclei, almost all the primordial isotopes of the alkali metals are odd–even (the exceptions being the light stable isotope lithium-6 and the long-lived [radioisotope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioisotope "Radioisotope") potassium-40). For a given odd mass number, there can be only a single [beta-stable nuclide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta-decay_stable_isobars "Beta-decay stable isobars"), since there is not a difference in binding energy between even–odd and odd–even comparable to that between even–even and odd–odd, leaving other nuclides of the same mass number ([isobars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isobar_\(nuclide\) "Isobar (nuclide)")) free to [beta decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_decay "Beta decay") toward the lowest-mass nuclide. An effect of the instability of an odd number of either type of nucleons is that odd-numbered elements, such as the alkali metals, tend to have fewer stable isotopes than even-numbered elements. Of the 26 [monoisotopic elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoisotopic_element "Monoisotopic element") that have only a single stable isotope, all but one have an odd atomic number and all but one also have an even number of neutrons. [Beryllium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryllium "Beryllium") is the single exception to both rules, due to its low atomic number.[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Lide02-99)

All of the alkali metals except lithium and caesium have at least one naturally occurring [radioisotope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioisotope "Radioisotope"): [sodium-22](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium-22 "Sodium-22") and [sodium-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium-24 "Sodium-24") are [trace radioisotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trace_radioisotope "Trace radioisotope") produced [cosmogenically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmogenic "Cosmogenic"),[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100) potassium-40 and [rubidium-87](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium-87 "Rubidium-87") have very long [half-lives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-lives "Half-lives") and thus occur naturally,[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nuclideschart-101) and all [isotopes of francium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_francium "Isotopes of francium") are [radioactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive "Radioactive").[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nuclideschart-101) Caesium was also thought to be radioactive in the early 20th century,[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Patt1926-102)[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Kenn1908-103) although it has no naturally occurring radioisotopes.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nuclideschart-101) (Francium had not been discovered yet at that time.) The natural long-lived radioisotope of potassium, potassium-40, makes up about 0.012% of natural potassium,[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104) and thus natural potassium is weakly radioactive. This natural radioactivity became a basis for a mistaken claim of the discovery for element 87 (the next alkali metal after caesium) in 1925.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fontani-35)[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vanderkrogt-Fr-36) Natural rubidium is similarly slightly radioactive, with 27.83% being the long-lived radioisotope rubidium-87.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74 

[Caesium-137](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium-137 "Caesium-137"), with a half-life of 30.17 years, is one of the two principal [medium-lived fission products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medium-lived_fission_product "Medium-lived fission product"), along with [strontium-90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strontium-90 "Strontium-90"), which are responsible for most of the [radioactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactivity "Radioactivity") of [spent nuclear fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spent_nuclear_fuel "Spent nuclear fuel") after several years of cooling, up to several hundred years after use. It constitutes most of the radioactivity still left from the [Chernobyl accident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_accident "Chernobyl accident"). Caesium-137 undergoes high-energy beta decay and eventually becomes stable [barium-137](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barium-137 "Barium-137"). It is a strong emitter of gamma radiation. Caesium-137 has a very low rate of neutron capture and cannot be feasibly disposed of in this way, but must be allowed to decay.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cs-137-105) Caesium-137 has been used as a [tracer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flow_tracer "Flow tracer") in hydrologic studies, analogous to the use of [tritium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tritium "Tritium").[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106) Small amounts of [caesium-134](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium-134 "Caesium-134") and caesium-137 were released into the environment during nearly all [nuclear weapon tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapon_test "Nuclear weapon test") and some [nuclear accidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_accident "Nuclear accident"), most notably the [Goiânia accident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goi%C3%A2nia_accident "Goiânia accident") and the [Chernobyl disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster "Chernobyl disaster"). As of 2005, caesium-137 is the principal source of radiation in the [zone of alienation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zone_of_alienation "Zone of alienation") around the [Chernobyl nuclear power plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_nuclear_power_plant "Chernobyl nuclear power plant").[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-IAEA-107) Its chemical properties as one of the alkali metals make it one of the most problematic of the short-to-medium-lifetime fission products because it easily moves and spreads in nature due to the high water solubility of its salts, and is taken up by the body, which mistakes it for its essential congeners sodium and potassium.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RPD-108): 114 

## Periodic trends

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Periodic trends")]

The alkali metals are more similar to each other than the elements in any other [group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_\(periodic_table\) "Group (periodic table)") are to each other.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) For instance, when moving down the table, all known alkali metals show increasing [atomic radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radius "Atomic radius"),[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) decreasing [electronegativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronegativity "Electronegativity"),[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) increasing [reactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactivity_\(chemistry\) "Reactivity (chemistry)"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and decreasing melting and boiling points[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) as well as heats of fusion and vaporisation.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75  In general, their [densities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Densities "Densities") increase when moving down the table, with the exception that potassium is less dense than sodium.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)

### Atomic and ionic radii

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Atomic and ionic radii")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Effective_Nuclear_Charge.svg/250px-Effective_Nuclear_Charge.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Effective_Nuclear_Charge.svg)

[Effective nuclear charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_nuclear_charge "Effective nuclear charge") on an atomic electron

The [atomic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radii "Atomic radii") of the alkali metals increase going down the group.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) Because of the [shielding effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shielding_effect "Shielding effect"), when an atom has more than one [electron shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_shell "Electron shell"), each electron feels electric repulsion from the other electrons as well as electric attraction from the nucleus.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-shielding-109) In the alkali metals, the [outermost electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valence_electron "Valence electron") only feels a net charge of +1, as some of the [nuclear charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_charge "Nuclear charge") (which is equal to the [atomic number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_number "Atomic number")) is cancelled by the inner electrons; the number of inner electrons of an alkali metal is always one less than the nuclear charge. Therefore, the only factor which affects the atomic radius of the alkali metals is the number of electron shells. Since this number increases down the group, the atomic radius must also increase down the group.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)

The [ionic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_radii "Ionic radii") of the alkali metals are much smaller than their atomic radii. This is because the outermost electron of the alkali metals is in a different [electron shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_shell "Electron shell") than the inner electrons, and thus when it is removed the resulting atom has one fewer electron shell and is smaller. Additionally, the [effective nuclear charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_nuclear_charge "Effective nuclear charge") has increased, and thus the electrons are attracted more strongly towards the nucleus and the ionic radius decreases.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)

### First ionisation energy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: First ionisation energy")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/First_Ionization_Energy_blocks.svg/590px-First_Ionization_Energy_blocks.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:First_Ionization_Energy_blocks.svg)

Periodic trend for ionisation energy: each period begins at a minimum for the alkali metals, and ends at a maximum for the [noble gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noble_gas "Noble gas"). Predicted values are used for elements beyond 104.

The first [ionisation energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionisation_energy "Ionisation energy") of an [element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_element "Chemical element") or [molecule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecule "Molecule") is the energy required to move the most loosely held electron from one [mole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_\(unit\) "Mole (unit)") of gaseous atoms of the element or molecules to form one mole of gaseous ions with [electric charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_charge "Electric charge") +1. The factors affecting the first ionisation energy are the [nuclear charge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_charge "Nuclear charge"), the amount of [shielding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shielding_effect "Shielding effect") by the inner electrons and the distance from the most loosely held electron from the nucleus, which is always an outer electron in [main group elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_group_element "Main group element"). The first two factors change the effective nuclear charge the most loosely held electron feels. Since the outermost electron of alkali metals always feels the same effective nuclear charge (+1), the only factor which affects the first ionisation energy is the distance from the outermost electron to the nucleus. Since this distance increases down the group, the outermost electron feels less attraction from the nucleus and thus the first ionisation energy decreases.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) This trend is broken in francium due to the [relativistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_quantum_chemistry "Relativistic quantum chemistry") stabilisation and contraction of the 7s orbital, bringing francium's valence electron closer to the nucleus than would be expected from non-relativistic calculations. This makes francium's outermost electron feel more attraction from the nucleus, increasing its first ionisation energy slightly beyond that of caesium.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729 

The second ionisation energy of the alkali metals is much higher than the first as the second-most loosely held electron is part of a fully filled [electron shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_shell "Electron shell") and is thus difficult to remove.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)

### Reactivity

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Reactivity")]

The [reactivities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactivity_\(chemistry\) "Reactivity (chemistry)") of the alkali metals increase going down the group. This is the result of a combination of two factors: the first ionisation energies and [atomisation energies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomisation_energy "Atomisation energy") of the alkali metals. Because the first ionisation energy of the alkali metals decreases down the group, it is easier for the outermost electron to be removed from the atom and participate in [chemical reactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_reaction "Chemical reaction"), thus increasing reactivity down the group. The atomisation energy measures the strength of the [metallic bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_bond "Metallic bond") of an element, which falls down the group as the atoms increase in [radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_radius "Atomic radius") and thus the metallic bond must increase in length, making the [delocalised electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delocalised_electrons "Delocalised electrons") further away from the attraction of the nuclei of the heavier alkali metals. Adding the atomisation and first ionisation energies gives a quantity closely related to (but not equal to) the [activation energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activation_energy "Activation energy") of the reaction of an alkali metal with another substance. This quantity decreases going down the group, and so does the activation energy; thus, chemical reactions can occur faster and the reactivity increases down the group.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkaliwater-110)

### Electronegativity

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Electronegativity")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Periodic_variation_of_Pauling_electronegativities.svg/280px-Periodic_variation_of_Pauling_electronegativities.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Periodic_variation_of_Pauling_electronegativities.svg)
