Periodic variation of Pauling electronegativities as one descends the [main groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_group_element "Main group element") of the periodic table from the [second](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_2_element "Period 2 element") to the [sixth period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_6_element "Period 6 element").

[Electronegativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronegativity "Electronegativity") is a [chemical property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_property "Chemical property") that describes the tendency of an [atom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atom "Atom") or a [functional group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional_group "Functional group") to attract [electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron "Electron") (or [electron density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_density "Electron density")) towards itself.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-definition-111) If the bond between [sodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium") and [chlorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorine "Chlorine") in [sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride") were [covalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent "Covalent"), the pair of shared electrons would be attracted to the chlorine because the effective nuclear charge on the outer electrons is +7 in chlorine but is only +1 in sodium. The electron pair is attracted so close to the chlorine atom that they are practically transferred to the chlorine atom (an [ionic bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_bond "Ionic bond")). However, if the sodium atom was replaced by a lithium atom, the electrons will not be attracted as close to the chlorine atom as before because the lithium atom is smaller, making the electron pair more strongly attracted to the closer effective nuclear charge from lithium. Hence, the larger alkali metal atoms (further down the group) will be less electronegative as the bonding pair is less strongly attracted towards them. As mentioned previously, francium is expected to be an exception.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)

Because of the higher electronegativity of lithium, some of its compounds have a more covalent character. For example, [lithium iodide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_iodide "Lithium iodide") (LiI) will dissolve in [organic solvents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_solvent "Organic solvent"), a property of most covalent compounds.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) [Lithium fluoride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_fluoride "Lithium fluoride") (LiF) is the only [alkali halide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_halide "Alkali halide") that is not soluble in water,[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) and [lithium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_hydroxide "Lithium hydroxide") (LiOH) is the only [alkali metal hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_metal_hydroxide "Alkali metal hydroxide") that is not [deliquescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deliquescent "Deliquescent").[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)

### Melting and boiling points

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Melting and boiling points")]

The [melting point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_point "Melting point") of a substance is the point where it changes [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_matter "State of matter") from solid to liquid while the [boiling point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boiling_point "Boiling point") of a substance (in liquid state) is the point where the [vapour pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vapour_pressure "Vapour pressure") of the liquid equals the environmental pressure surrounding the liquid[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112)[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113) and all the liquid changes state to gas. As a metal is heated to its melting point, the [metallic bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_bond "Metallic bond") keeping the atoms in place weaken so that the atoms can move around, and the metallic bonds eventually break completely at the metal's boiling point.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-metallic-bonding-114) Therefore, the falling melting and boiling points of the alkali metals indicate that the strength of the metallic bonds of the alkali metals decreases down the group.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) This is because metal atoms are held together by the electromagnetic attraction from the positive ions to the delocalised electrons.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-metallic-bonding-114) As the atoms increase in size going down the group (because their atomic radius increases), the nuclei of the ions move further away from the delocalised electrons and hence the metallic bond becomes weaker so that the metal can more easily melt and boil, thus lowering the melting and boiling points.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) The increased nuclear charge is not a relevant factor due to the shielding effect.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81)

### Density

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Density")]

The alkali metals all have the same [crystal structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_structure "Crystal structure") ([body-centred cubic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body-centred_cubic "Body-centred cubic"))[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14) and thus the only relevant factors are the number of atoms that can fit into a certain volume and the mass of one of the atoms, since density is defined as mass per unit volume. The first factor depends on the volume of the atom and thus the atomic radius, which increases going down the group; thus, the volume of an alkali metal atom increases going down the group. The mass of an alkali metal atom also increases going down the group. Thus, the trend for the densities of the alkali metals depends on their atomic weights and atomic radii; if figures for these two factors are known, the ratios between the densities of the alkali metals can then be calculated. The resultant trend is that the densities of the alkali metals increase down the table, with an exception at potassium. Due to having the lowest atomic weight and the largest atomic radius of all the elements in their periods, the alkali metals are the least dense metals in the periodic table.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemguide-81) Lithium, sodium, and potassium are the only three metals in the periodic table that are less dense than water:[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) in fact, lithium is the least dense known solid at [room temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Room_temperature "Room temperature").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 75 

## Compounds

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Compounds")]

The alkali metals form complete series of compounds with all usually encountered anions, which well illustrate group trends. These compounds can be described as involving the alkali metals losing electrons to acceptor species and forming monopositive ions.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 79  This description is most accurate for alkali halides and becomes less and less accurate as cationic and anionic charge increase, and as the anion becomes larger and more polarisable. For instance, [ionic bonding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_bond "Ionic bond") gives way to [metallic bonding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_bond "Metallic bond") along the series NaCl, Na2O, Na2S, Na3P, Na3As, Na3Sb, Na3Bi, Na.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 81 

### [Hydroxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxides "Hydroxides")

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Hydroxides")]

| External videos                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |   |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | - |
| ![video icon](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/16px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png) [Alkali Metals – 20 Reactions of the alkali metals with water](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eaChisV5uR0), conducted by [The Royal Society of Chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Royal_Society_of_Chemistry "The Royal Society of Chemistry") |   |

[![A large orange-yellow explosion](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Large_Sodium_Explosion.jpg/220px-Large_Sodium_Explosion.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Large_Sodium_Explosion.jpg)

A reaction of 3 [pounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pound_\(mass\) "Pound (mass)") (≈ 1.4 kg) of sodium with water

All the alkali metals react vigorously or explosively with cold water, producing an [aqueous solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueous_solution "Aqueous solution") of a strongly [basic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_\(chemistry\) "Base (chemistry)") alkali metal [hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxide "Hydroxide") and releasing hydrogen gas.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkaliwater-110) This reaction becomes more vigorous going down the group: lithium reacts steadily with [effervescence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effervescence "Effervescence"), but sodium and potassium can ignite, and rubidium and caesium sink in water and generate hydrogen gas so rapidly that shock waves form in the water that may shatter glass containers.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) When an alkali metal is dropped into water, it produces an explosion, of which there are two separate stages. The metal reacts with the water first, breaking the hydrogen bonds in the water and producing [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen "Hydrogen") gas; this takes place faster for the more reactive heavier alkali metals. Second, the heat generated by the first part of the reaction often ignites the hydrogen gas, causing it to burn explosively into the surrounding air. This secondary hydrogen gas explosion produces the visible flame above the bowl of water, lake or other body of water, not the initial reaction of the metal with water (which tends to happen mostly under water).[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalibangs-84) The alkali metal hydroxides are the most basic known hydroxides.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 87 

Recent research has suggested that the explosive behavior of alkali metals in water is driven by a [Coulomb explosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coulomb_explosion "Coulomb explosion") rather than solely by rapid generation of hydrogen itself.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-coulomb-115) All alkali metals melt as a part of the reaction with water. Water molecules ionise the bare metallic surface of the liquid metal, leaving a positively charged metal surface and negatively charged water ions. The attraction between the charged metal and water ions will rapidly increase the surface area, causing an exponential increase of ionisation. When the repulsive forces within the liquid metal surface exceeds the forces of the surface tension, it vigorously explodes.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-coulomb-115)

The hydroxides themselves are the most basic hydroxides known, reacting with acids to give salts and with alcohols to give [oligomeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oligomer "Oligomer") [alkoxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkoxide "Alkoxide"). They easily react with [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide "Carbon dioxide") to form [carbonates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonate "Carbonate") or [bicarbonates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicarbonate "Bicarbonate"), or with [hydrogen sulfide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_sulfide "Hydrogen sulfide") to form [sulfides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfide "Sulfide") or [bisulfides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisulfide "Bisulfide"), and may be used to separate [thiols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thiol "Thiol") from petroleum. They react with amphoteric oxides: for example, the oxides of [aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium_oxide "Aluminium oxide"), [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc_oxide "Zinc oxide"), [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin\(IV\)_oxide "Tin(IV) oxide"), and [lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_dioxide "Lead dioxide") react with the alkali metal hydroxides to give aluminates, zincates, stannates, and plumbates. [Silicon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide "Silicon dioxide") is acidic, and thus the alkali metal hydroxides can also attack [silicate glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicate_glass "Silicate glass").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 87 

### Intermetallic compounds

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Intermetallic compounds")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/NaK_alloy.jpg/220px-NaK_alloy.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NaK_alloy.jpg)

Liquid NaK alloy at room temperature

The alkali metals form many [intermetallic compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermetallic_compound "Intermetallic compound") with each other and the elements from groups [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal") to [13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron_group "Boron group") in the periodic table of varying stoichiometries,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 81  such as the [sodium amalgams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_amalgam "Sodium amalgam") with [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\) "Mercury (element)"), including Na5Hg8 and Na3Hg.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-116) Some of these have ionic characteristics: taking the alloys with gold, the most electronegative of metals, as an example, NaAu and KAu are metallic, but RbAu and [CsAu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CsAu "CsAu") are semiconductors.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 81  [NaK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NaK "NaK") is an alloy of sodium and potassium that is very useful because it is liquid at room temperature, although precautions must be taken due to its extreme reactivity towards water and air. The [eutectic mixture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eutectic_mixture "Eutectic mixture") melts at −12.6 °C.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-basf-ds-NaK-117) An alloy of 41% caesium, 47% sodium, and 12% potassium has the lowest known melting point of any metal or alloy, −78 °C.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-caesium-26)

### Compounds with the group 13 elements

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Compounds with the group 13 elements")]

The intermetallic compounds of the alkali metals with the heavier group 13 elements (aluminium, [gallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallium "Gallium"), [indium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indium "Indium"), and [thallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thallium "Thallium")), such as NaTl, are poor [conductors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_conductor "Electrical conductor") or [semiconductors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor "Semiconductor"), unlike the normal alloys with the preceding elements, implying that the alkali metal involved has lost an electron to the [Zintl anions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zintl_phase "Zintl phase") involved.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Sevov-118) Nevertheless, while the elements in group 14 and beyond tend to form discrete anionic clusters, group 13 elements tend to form polymeric ions with the alkali metal cations located between the giant ionic lattice. For example, NaTl consists of a polymeric anion (—Tl−—)n with a covalent [diamond cubic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond_cubic "Diamond cubic") structure with Na+ ions located between the anionic lattice. The larger alkali metals cannot fit similarly into an anionic lattice and tend to force the heavier group 13 elements to form anionic clusters.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Kauzlarich-119)

[Boron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron "Boron") is a special case, being the only nonmetal in group 13. The alkali metal [borides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boride "Boride") tend to be boron-rich, involving appreciable boron–boron bonding involving [deltahedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deltahedron "Deltahedron") structures,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 147–8  and are thermally unstable due to the alkali metals having a very high [vapour pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vapour_pressure "Vapour pressure") at elevated temperatures. This makes direct synthesis problematic because the alkali metals do not react with boron below 700 °C, and thus this must be accomplished in sealed containers with the alkali metal in excess. Furthermore, exceptionally in this group, reactivity with boron decreases down the group: lithium reacts completely at 700 °C, but sodium at 900 °C and potassium not until 1200 °C, and the reaction is instantaneous for lithium but takes hours for potassium. Rubidium and caesium borides have not even been characterised. Various phases are known, such as LiB10, NaB6, NaB15, and KB6.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-120)[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121) Under high pressure the boron–boron bonding in the lithium borides changes from following [Wade's rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wade%27s_rules "Wade's rules") to forming Zintl anions like the rest of group 13.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-122)

### Compounds with the group 14 elements

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Compounds with the group 14 elements")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-A.png/150px-Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-A.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-A.png)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-B.png/150px-Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-B.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Potassium-graphite-xtal-3D-SF-B.png)

Side *(left)* and top *(right)* views of the [graphite intercalation compound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphite_intercalation_compound "Graphite intercalation compound") KC8

Lithium and sodium react with [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon "Carbon") to form [acetylides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylide "Acetylide"), Li2C2 and Na2C2, which can also be obtained by reaction of the metal with [acetylene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylene "Acetylene"). Potassium, rubidium, and caesium react with [graphite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphite "Graphite"); their atoms are [intercalated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercalation_\(chemistry\) "Intercalation (chemistry)") between the hexagonal graphite layers, forming [graphite intercalation compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphite_intercalation_compound "Graphite intercalation compound") of formulae MC60 (dark grey, almost black), MC48 (dark grey, almost black), MC36 (blue), MC24 (steel blue), and MC8 (bronze) (M = K, Rb, or Cs). These compounds are over 200 times more electrically conductive than pure graphite, suggesting that the valence electron of the alkali metal is transferred to the graphite layers (e.g. M+C−8).[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Upon heating of KC8, the elimination of potassium atoms results in the conversion in sequence to KC24, KC36, KC48 and finally KC60. KC8 is a very strong [reducing agent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reducing_agent "Reducing agent") and is pyrophoric and explodes on contact with water.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-InorgChem-123)[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124) While the larger alkali metals (K, Rb, and Cs) initially form MC8, the smaller ones initially form MC6, and indeed they require reaction of the metals with graphite at high temperatures around 500 °C to form.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-cac6-125) Apart from this, the alkali metals are such strong reducing agents that they can even reduce [buckminsterfullerene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckminsterfullerene "Buckminsterfullerene") to produce solid [fullerides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulleride "Fulleride") M*n*C60; sodium, potassium, rubidium, and caesium can form fullerides where *n* = 2, 3, 4, or 6, and rubidium and caesium additionally can achieve *n* = 1.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 285 

When the alkali metals react with the heavier elements in the [carbon group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_group "Carbon group") ([silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon "Silicon"), [germanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanium "Germanium"), [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin "Tin"), and lead), ionic substances with cage-like structures are formed, such as the [silicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicide "Silicide") M4[Si](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon "Silicon")4 (M = K, Rb, or Cs), which contains M+ and tetrahedral Si4−4 ions.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) The chemistry of alkali metal [germanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanide "Germanide"), involving the germanide ion [Ge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanium "Germanium")4− and other cluster ([Zintl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zintl_ion "Zintl ion")) ions such as Ge2−4, Ge4−9, Ge2−9, and \[(Ge9)2]6−, is largely analogous to that of the corresponding silicides.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 393  Alkali metal [stannides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stannide "Stannide") are mostly ionic, sometimes with the stannide ion ([Sn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin "Tin")4−),[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Kauzlarich-119) and sometimes with more complex Zintl ions such as Sn4−9, which appears in tetrapotassium nonastannide (K4Sn9).[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hoch-126) The monatomic [plumbide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plumbide "Plumbide") ion ([Pb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead "Lead")4−) is unknown, and indeed its formation is predicted to be energetically unfavourable; alkali metal plumbides have complex Zintl ions, such as Pb4−9. These alkali metal germanides, stannides, and plumbides may be produced by reducing germanium, tin, and lead with sodium metal in liquid ammonia.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 394 

### Nitrides and pnictides

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Nitrides and pnictides")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Lithium-nitride-xtal-CM-3D-polyhedra.png/220px-Lithium-nitride-xtal-CM-3D-polyhedra.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lithium-nitride-xtal-CM-3D-polyhedra.png)

[Unit cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_cell "Unit cell") [ball-and-stick model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball-and-stick_model "Ball-and-stick model") of [lithium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_nitride "Lithium nitride").[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-127) On the basis of size a [tetrahedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrahedral "Tetrahedral") structure would be expected, but that would be geometrically impossible: thus lithium nitride takes on this unique crystal structure.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76 

Lithium, the lightest of the alkali metals, is the only alkali metal which reacts with [nitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen "Nitrogen") at [standard conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_conditions "Standard conditions"), and its [nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitride "Nitride") is the only stable alkali metal nitride. Nitrogen is an [unreactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unreactive "Unreactive") gas because breaking the strong [triple bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_bond "Triple bond") in the [dinitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinitrogen "Dinitrogen") molecule (N2) requires a lot of energy. The formation of an alkali metal nitride would consume the ionisation energy of the alkali metal (forming M+ ions), the energy required to break the triple bond in N2 and the formation of N3− ions, and all the energy released from the formation of an alkali metal nitride is from the [lattice energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lattice_energy "Lattice energy") of the alkali metal nitride. The lattice energy is maximised with small, highly charged ions; the alkali metals do not form highly charged ions, only forming ions with a charge of +1, so only lithium, the smallest alkali metal, can release enough lattice energy to make the reaction with nitrogen [exothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exothermic "Exothermic"), forming [lithium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_nitride "Lithium nitride"). The reactions of the other alkali metals with nitrogen would not release enough lattice energy and would thus be [endothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endothermic "Endothermic"), so they do not form nitrides at standard conditions.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalireact-94) [Sodium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_nitride "Sodium nitride") (Na3N) and [potassium nitride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_nitride "Potassium nitride") (K3N), while existing, are extremely unstable, being prone to decomposing back into their constituent elements, and cannot be produced by reacting the elements with each other at standard conditions.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jansen1-128)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jansen2-129) Steric hindrance forbids the existence of rubidium or caesium nitride.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 417  However, sodium and potassium form colourless [azide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azide "Azide") salts involving the linear N−3 anion; due to the large size of the alkali metal cations, they are thermally stable enough to be able to melt before decomposing.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 417 

All the alkali metals react readily with [phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorus "Phosphorus") and [arsenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenic "Arsenic") to form [phosphides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphide "Phosphide") and [arsenides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenide "Arsenide") with the formula M3Pn (where M represents an alkali metal and Pn represents a [pnictogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pnictogen "Pnictogen") – phosphorus, arsenic, [antimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimony "Antimony"), or [bismuth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismuth "Bismuth")). This is due to the greater size of the P3− and As3− ions, so that less lattice energy needs to be released for the salts to form.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) These are not the only phosphides and arsenides of the alkali metals: for example, potassium has nine different known phosphides, with formulae K3P, K4P3, K5P4, KP, K4P6, K3P7, K3P11, KP10.3, and KP15.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Schnering-130) While most metals form arsenides, only the alkali and alkaline earth metals form mostly ionic arsenides. The structure of Na3As is complex with unusually short Na–Na distances of 328–330 pm which are shorter than in sodium metal, and this indicates that even with these electropositive metals the bonding cannot be straightforwardly ionic.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14) Other alkali metal arsenides not conforming to the formula M3As are known, such as LiAs, which has a metallic lustre and electrical conductivity indicating the presence of some [metallic bonding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_bond "Metallic bond").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14) The [antimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antimonide "Antimonide") are unstable and reactive as the Sb3− ion is a strong reducing agent; reaction of them with acids form the toxic and unstable gas [stibine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stibine "Stibine") (SbH3).[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-131) Indeed, they have some metallic properties, and the alkali metal antimonides of stoichiometry MSb involve antimony atoms bonded in a spiral Zintl structure.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132) [Bismuthides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismuthide "Bismuthide") are not even wholly ionic; they are [intermetallic compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermetallic_compound "Intermetallic compound") containing partially metallic and partially ionic bonds.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133)

### Oxides and chalcogenides

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Oxides and chalcogenides")]

See also: [Alkali metal oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_metal_oxide "Alkali metal oxide")

[![The ball-and-stick diagram shows two regular octahedra which are connected to each other by one face. All nine vertices of the structure are purple spheres representing rubidium, and at the centre of each octahedron is a small red sphere representing oxygen.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Rb9O2_cluster.png/150px-Rb9O2_cluster.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rb9O2_cluster.png)

Rb9O2 cluster, composed of two regular [octahedra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octahedra "Octahedra") connected to each other by one face

[![The ball-and-stick diagram shows three regular octahedra where each octahedron is connected to both of the others by one face each. All three octahedra have one edge in common. All eleven vertices of the structure are violet spheres representing caesium, and at the centre of each octahedron is a small red sphere representing oxygen.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Cs11O3_cluster.png/150px-Cs11O3_cluster.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cs11O3_cluster.png)

Cs11O3 cluster, composed of three regular octahedra where each octahedron is connected to both of the others by one face each. All three octahedra have one edge in common.

All the alkali metals react vigorously with [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen") at standard conditions. They form various types of oxides, such as simple [oxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxide "Oxide") (containing the O2− ion), [peroxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peroxide "Peroxide") (containing the O2−2 ion, where there is a [single bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_bond "Single bond") between the two oxygen atoms), [superoxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superoxide "Superoxide") (containing the O−2 ion), and many others. Lithium burns in air to form [lithium oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_oxide "Lithium oxide"), but sodium reacts with oxygen to form a mixture of [sodium oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_oxide "Sodium oxide") and [sodium peroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_peroxide "Sodium peroxide"). Potassium forms a mixture of [potassium peroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_peroxide "Potassium peroxide") and [potassium superoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_superoxide "Potassium superoxide"), while rubidium and caesium form the superoxide exclusively. Their reactivity increases going down the group: while lithium, sodium and potassium merely burn in air, rubidium and caesium are [pyrophoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrophoric "Pyrophoric") (spontaneously catch fire in air).[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalireact-94)

The smaller alkali metals tend to polarise the larger anions (the peroxide and superoxide) due to their small size. This attracts the electrons in the more complex anions towards one of its constituent oxygen atoms, forming an oxide ion and an oxygen atom. This causes lithium to form the oxide exclusively on reaction with oxygen at room temperature. This effect becomes drastically weaker for the larger sodium and potassium, allowing them to form the less stable peroxides. Rubidium and caesium, at the bottom of the group, are so large that even the least stable superoxides can form. Because the superoxide releases the most energy when formed, the superoxide is preferentially formed for the larger alkali metals where the more complex anions are not polarised. The oxides and peroxides for these alkali metals do exist, but do not form upon direct reaction of the metal with oxygen at standard conditions.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalireact-94) In addition, the small size of the Li+ and O2− ions contributes to their forming a stable ionic lattice structure. Under controlled conditions, however, all the alkali metals, with the exception of francium, are known to form their oxides, peroxides, and superoxides. The alkali metal peroxides and superoxides are powerful [oxidising agents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidising_agent "Oxidising agent"). [Sodium peroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_peroxide "Sodium peroxide") and [potassium superoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_superoxide "Potassium superoxide") react with [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide "Carbon dioxide") to form the alkali metal carbonate and oxygen gas, which allows them to be used in [submarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Submarine "Submarine") air purifiers; the presence of [water vapour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_vapour "Water vapour"), naturally present in breath, makes the removal of carbon dioxide by potassium superoxide even more efficient.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134) All the stable alkali metals except lithium can form red [ozonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozonide "Ozonide") (MO3) through low-temperature reaction of the powdered anhydrous hydroxide with [ozone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozone "Ozone"): the ozonides may be then extracted using liquid [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia"). They slowly decompose at standard conditions to the superoxides and oxygen, and hydrolyse immediately to the hydroxides when in contact with water.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 85  Potassium, rubidium, and caesium also form sesquioxides M2O3, which may be better considered peroxide disuperoxides, \[(M+)4(O2−2)(O−2)2].[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 85 

Rubidium and caesium can form a great variety of suboxides with the metals in formal oxidation states below +1.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 85  Rubidium can form Rb6O and Rb9O2 (copper-coloured) upon oxidation in air, while caesium forms an immense variety of oxides, such as the ozonide CsO3[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135)[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136) and several brightly coloured [suboxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suboxide "Suboxide"),[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Simon-137) such as Cs7O (bronze), Cs4O (red-violet), Cs11O3 (violet), Cs3O (dark green),[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-138) CsO, Cs3O2,[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139) as well as Cs7O2.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-141) The last of these may be heated under vacuum to generate Cs2O.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pubs.usgs-62)

The alkali metals can also react analogously with the heavier chalcogens ([sulfur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur "Sulfur"), [selenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenium "Selenium"), [tellurium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tellurium "Tellurium"), and [polonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonium "Polonium")), and all the alkali metal chalcogenides are known (with the exception of francium's). Reaction with an excess of the chalcogen can similarly result in lower chalcogenides, with chalcogen ions containing chains of the chalcogen atoms in question. For example, sodium can react with sulfur to form the [sulfide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfide "Sulfide") ([Na2S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_sulfide "Sodium sulfide")) and various [polysulfides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polysulfide "Polysulfide") with the formula Na2S*x* (*x* from 2 to 6), containing the S2−\
*x* ions.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Due to the basicity of the Se2− and Te2− ions, the alkali metal [selenides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenide "Selenide") and [tellurides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tellurides "Tellurides") are alkaline in solution; when reacted directly with selenium and tellurium, alkali metal polyselenides and polytellurides are formed along with the selenides and tellurides with the Se2−\
*x* and Te2−\
*x* ions.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-house2008-142) They may be obtained directly from the elements in liquid ammonia or when air is not present, and are colourless, water-soluble compounds that air oxidises quickly back to selenium or tellurium.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 766  The alkali metal [polonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polonide "Polonide") are all ionic compounds containing the Po2− ion; they are very chemically stable and can be produced by direct reaction of the elements at around 300–400 °C.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 766 [\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AEC-chem-143)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bagnall-144)

### Halides, hydrides, and pseudohalides

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=24 "Edit section: Halides, hydrides, and pseudohalides")]

Main article: [Alkali metal halide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_metal_halide "Alkali metal halide")

The alkali metals are among the most [electropositive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electropositive "Electropositive") elements on the periodic table and thus tend to [bond ionically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_bond "Ionic bond") to the most [electronegative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronegative "Electronegative") elements on the periodic table, the [halogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halogen "Halogen") ([fluorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorine "Fluorine"), [chlorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chlorine "Chlorine"), [bromine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bromine "Bromine"), [iodine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iodine "Iodine"), and [astatine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astatine "Astatine")), forming [salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salts "Salts") known as the alkali metal halides. The reaction is very vigorous and can sometimes result in explosions.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76  All twenty stable alkali metal halides are known; the unstable ones are not known, with the exception of sodium astatide, because of the great instability and rarity of astatine and francium. The most well-known of the twenty is certainly [sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride"), otherwise known as common salt. All of the stable alkali metal halides have the formula MX where M is an alkali metal and X is a halogen. They are all white ionic crystalline solids that have high melting points.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-alkalireact-94) All the alkali metal halides are [soluble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soluble "Soluble") in water except for [lithium fluoride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_fluoride "Lithium fluoride") (LiF), which is insoluble in water due to its very high [lattice enthalpy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lattice_enthalpy "Lattice enthalpy"). The high lattice enthalpy of lithium fluoride is due to the small sizes of the Li+ and F− ions, causing the [electrostatic interactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrostatic_interaction "Electrostatic interaction") between them to be strong:[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) a similar effect occurs for [magnesium fluoride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium_fluoride "Magnesium fluoride"), consistent with the diagonal relationship between lithium and magnesium.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 76 

The alkali metals also react similarly with hydrogen to form ionic alkali metal hydrides, where the [hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydride "Hydride") anion acts as a [pseudohalide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudohalogen "Pseudohalogen"): these are often used as reducing agents, producing hydrides, complex metal hydrides, or hydrogen gas.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 83 [\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Other pseudohalides are also known, notably the [cyanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyanide "Cyanide"). These are isostructural to the respective halides except for [lithium cyanide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_cyanide "Lithium cyanide"), indicating that the cyanide ions may rotate freely.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 322  Ternary alkali metal halide oxides, such as Na3ClO, K3BrO (yellow), Na4Br2O, Na4I2O, and K4Br2O, are also known.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 83  The polyhalides are rather unstable, although those of rubidium and caesium are greatly stabilised by the feeble polarising power of these extremely large cations.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 835 

### Coordination complexes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Coordination complexes")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/18-crown-6-potassium-3D-balls-A.png/150px-18-crown-6-potassium-3D-balls-A.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:18-crown-6-potassium-3D-balls-A.png)

[18-crown-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18-crown-6 "18-crown-6") coordinating a potassium ion

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Cryptate_of_potassium_cation.jpg/150px-Cryptate_of_potassium_cation.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cryptate_of_potassium_cation.jpg)

Structure of [2.2.2-Cryptand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2.2.2-Cryptand "2.2.2-Cryptand") encapsulating a potassium cation (purple). At crystalline state, obtained with an X-ray diffraction.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-145)

Alkali metal cations do not usually form [coordination complexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordination_complex "Coordination complex") with simple [Lewis bases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_base "Lewis base") due to their low charge of just +1 and their relatively large size; thus the Li+ ion forms most complexes and the heavier alkali metal ions form less and less (though exceptions occur for weak complexes).[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 90  Lithium in particular has a very rich coordination chemistry in which it exhibits [coordination numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordination_number "Coordination number") from 1 to 12, although octahedral hexacoordination is its preferred mode.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 90–1  In [aqueous solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueous_solution "Aqueous solution"), the alkali metal ions exist as octahedral hexahydrate complexes \[M(H2O)6]+, with the exception of the lithium ion, which due to its small size forms tetrahedral tetrahydrate complexes \[Li(H2O)4]+; the alkali metals form these complexes because their ions are attracted by electrostatic forces of attraction to the polar water molecules. Because of this, [anhydrous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anhydrous "Anhydrous") salts containing alkali metal cations are often used as [desiccants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desiccant "Desiccant").[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Alkali metals also readily form complexes with [crown ethers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_ether "Crown ether") (e.g. [12-crown-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12-crown-4 "12-crown-4") for Li+, [15-crown-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15-crown-5 "15-crown-5") for Na+, [18-crown-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18-crown-6 "18-crown-6") for K+, and [21-crown-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/21-crown-7 "21-crown-7") for Rb+) and [cryptands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptand "Cryptand") due to electrostatic attraction.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)

### Ammonia solutions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Ammonia solutions")]

The alkali metals dissolve slowly in liquid [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia"), forming ammoniacal solutions of solvated metal cation M+ and [solvated electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solvated_electron "Solvated electron") e−, which react to form hydrogen gas and the [alkali metal amide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metal_amide#Alkali_metal_amides "Metal amide") (MNH2, where M represents an alkali metal): this was first noted by [Humphry Davy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humphry_Davy "Humphry Davy") in 1809 and rediscovered by W. Weyl in 1864. The process may be speeded up by a [catalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalyst "Catalyst"). Similar solutions are formed by the heavy divalent [alkaline earth metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal") [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium"), [strontium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strontium "Strontium"), [barium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barium "Barium"), as well as the divalent [lanthanides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanthanide "Lanthanide"), [europium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europium "Europium") and [ytterbium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ytterbium "Ytterbium"). The amide salt is quite insoluble and readily precipitates out of solution, leaving intensely coloured ammonia solutions of the alkali metals. In 1907, [Charles A. Kraus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_A._Kraus "Charles A. Kraus") identified the colour as being due to the presence of [solvated electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solvated_electron "Solvated electron"), which contribute to the high electrical conductivity of these solutions. At low concentrations (below 3 M), the solution is dark blue and has ten times the conductivity of aqueous [sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride"); at higher concentrations (above 3 M), the solution is copper-coloured and has approximately the conductivity of liquid metals like [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\) "Mercury (element)").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14)[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-c\&w-146) In addition to the alkali metal amide salt and solvated electrons, such ammonia solutions also contain the alkali metal cation (M+), the neutral alkali metal atom (M), [diatomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diatomic "Diatomic") alkali metal molecules (M2) and alkali metal anions (M−). These are unstable and eventually become the more thermodynamically stable alkali metal amide and hydrogen gas. Solvated electrons are powerful [reducing agents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reducing_agent "Reducing agent") and are often used in chemical synthesis.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)

### Organometallic

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Organometallic")]

#### Organolithium

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Organolithium")]

Main article: [Organolithium reagent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organolithium_reagent "Organolithium reagent")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Butyllithium-hexamer-from-xtal-3D-balls-A.png/250px-Butyllithium-hexamer-from-xtal-3D-balls-A.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Butyllithium-hexamer-from-xtal-3D-balls-A.png)

Structure of the octahedral [*n*-butyllithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-butyllithium "N-butyllithium") hexamer, (C4H9Li)6.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-147) The aggregates are held together by delocalised covalent bonds between lithium and the terminal carbon of the butyl chain.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-148) There is no direct lithium–lithium bonding in any organolithium compound.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 264 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Phenyllithium-chain-from-xtal-Mercury-3D-balls.png/250px-Phenyllithium-chain-from-xtal-Mercury-3D-balls.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Phenyllithium-chain-from-xtal-Mercury-3D-balls.png)

Solid [phenyllithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenyllithium "Phenyllithium") forms monoclinic crystals that can be described as consisting of dimeric Li2([C6H5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenyl_group "Phenyl group"))2 subunits. The lithium atoms and the *[ipso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arene_substitution_pattern "Arene substitution pattern")* carbons of the phenyl rings form a planar four-membered ring. The plane of the phenyl groups is perpendicular to the plane of this Li2C2 ring. Additional strong intermolecular bonding occurs between these phenyllithium dimers and the π electrons of the phenyl groups in the adjacent dimers, resulting in an infinite polymeric ladder structure.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-149)

Being the smallest alkali metal, lithium forms the widest variety of and most stable [organometallic compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organometallic_compound "Organometallic compound"), which are bonded covalently. [Organolithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organolithium "Organolithium") compounds are electrically non-conducting volatile solids or liquids that melt at low temperatures, and tend to form [oligomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oligomer "Oligomer") with the structure (RLi)*x* where R is the organic group. As the electropositive nature of lithium puts most of the [charge density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charge_density "Charge density") of the bond on the carbon atom, effectively creating a [carbanion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbanion "Carbanion"), organolithium compounds are extremely powerful [bases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_\(chemistry\) "Base (chemistry)") and [nucleophiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_nucleophile "Carbon nucleophile"). For use as bases, [butyllithiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butyllithium "Butyllithium") are often used and are commercially available. An example of an organolithium compound is [methyllithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methyllithium "Methyllithium") ((CH3Li)*x*), which exists in tetrameric (*x* = 4, tetrahedral) and hexameric (*x* = 6, octahedral) forms.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Brown1957-150) Organolithium compounds, especially *n*-butyllithium, are useful reagents in organic synthesis, as might be expected given lithium's diagonal relationship with magnesium, which plays an important role in the [Grignard reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grignard_reaction "Grignard reaction").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 102  For example, alkyllithiums and aryllithiums may be used to synthesise [aldehydes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldehyde "Aldehyde") and [ketones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketone "Ketone") by reaction with metal [carbonyls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonyl "Carbonyl"). The reaction with [nickel tetracarbonyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel_tetracarbonyl "Nickel tetracarbonyl"), for example, proceeds through an unstable acyl nickel carbonyl complex which then undergoes [electrophilic substitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrophilic_substitution "Electrophilic substitution") to give the desired aldehyde (using H+ as the electrophile) or ketone (using an alkyl halide) product.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 105 

- - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {LiR\ +\ Ni(CO)4\ \longrightarrow Li^{+}\[RCONi(CO)3\]^{-}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b1783ddc7161bab3c63eb4c179496f65fc6ece16)
  - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {Li^{+}\[RCONi(CO)3\]^{-}->\[{\ce {H^{+}}}\]\[{\ce {solvent}}\]\ Li^{+}\ +\ RCHO\ +\ \[(solvent)Ni(CO)3\]}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/048a72890ac48383f7155a9f800660ab7b919d38)
  - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {Li^{+}\[RCONi(CO)3\]^{-}->\[{\ce {R^{'}Br}}\]\[{\ce {solvent}}\]\ Li^{+}\ +\ RR^{'}CO\ +\ \[(solvent)Ni(CO)3\]}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7ccae3545edde30fdaec9ebc9a5dde6cbd28d06d)

Alkyllithiums and aryllithiums may also react with *N*,*N*-disubstituted [amides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amide "Amide") to give aldehydes and ketones, and symmetrical ketones by reacting with [carbon monoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_monoxide "Carbon monoxide"). They thermally decompose to eliminate a β-hydrogen, producing [alkenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkene "Alkene") and [lithium hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_hydride "Lithium hydride"): another route is the reaction of [ethers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ether "Ether") with alkyl- and aryllithiums that act as strong bases.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 105  In non-polar solvents, aryllithiums react as the carbanions they effectively are, turning carbon dioxide to aromatic [carboxylic acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carboxylic_acid "Carboxylic acid") (ArCO2H) and aryl ketones to tertiary carbinols (Ar'2C(Ar)OH). Finally, they may be used to synthesise other organometallic compounds through metal-halogen exchange.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 106 

#### Heavier alkali metals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Heavier alkali metals")]

Unlike the organolithium compounds, the organometallic compounds of the heavier alkali metals are predominantly ionic. The application of [organosodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organosodium "Organosodium") compounds in chemistry is limited in part due to competition from [organolithium compounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organolithium_compound "Organolithium compound"), which are commercially available and exhibit more convenient reactivity. The principal organosodium compound of commercial importance is [sodium cyclopentadienide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_cyclopentadienide "Sodium cyclopentadienide"). [Sodium tetraphenylborate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_tetraphenylborate "Sodium tetraphenylborate") can also be classified as an organosodium compound since in the solid state sodium is bound to the aryl groups. Organometallic compounds of the higher alkali metals are even more reactive than organosodium compounds and of limited utility. A notable reagent is [Schlosser's base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schlosser%27s_base "Schlosser's base"), a mixture of [*n*-butyllithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-Butyllithium "N-Butyllithium") and [potassium *tert*-butoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_tert-butoxide "Potassium tert-butoxide"). This reagent reacts with [propene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propene "Propene") to form the compound [allylpotassium](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Allylpotassium\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Allylpotassium (page does not exist)") (KCH2CHCH2). [*cis*-2-Butene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cis-2-butene "Cis-2-butene") and [*trans*-2-butene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-2-butene "Trans-2-butene") equilibrate when in contact with alkali metals. Whereas [isomerisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isomerisation "Isomerisation") is fast with lithium and sodium, it is slow with the heavier alkali metals. The heavier alkali metals also favour the [sterically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steric_hindrance "Steric hindrance") congested conformation.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151) Several crystal structures of organopotassium compounds have been reported, establishing that they, like the sodium compounds, are polymeric.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Klett-152) Organosodium, organopotassium, organorubidium and organocaesium compounds are all mostly ionic and are insoluble (or nearly so) in nonpolar solvents.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71)

Alkyl and aryl derivatives of sodium and potassium tend to react with air. They cause the cleavage of [ethers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ether "Ether"), generating alkoxides. Unlike alkyllithium compounds, alkylsodiums and alkylpotassiums cannot be made by reacting the metals with alkyl halides because [Wurtz coupling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wurtz_coupling "Wurtz coupling") occurs:[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 265 

- RM + R'X → R–R' + MX

As such, they have to be made by reacting [alkylmercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organomercury_compound "Organomercury compound") compounds with sodium or potassium metal in inert hydrocarbon solvents. While methylsodium forms tetramers like methyllithium, methylpotassium is more ionic and has the [nickel arsenide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel_arsenide "Nickel arsenide") structure with discrete methyl anions and potassium cations.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 265 

The alkali metals and their hydrides react with acidic hydrocarbons, for example [cyclopentadienes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclopentadiene "Cyclopentadiene") and terminal alkynes, to give salts. Liquid ammonia, ether, or hydrocarbon solvents are used, the most common of which being [tetrahydrofuran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrahydrofuran "Tetrahydrofuran"). The most important of these compounds is [sodium cyclopentadienide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_cyclopentadienide "Sodium cyclopentadienide"), NaC5H5, an important precursor to many transition metal cyclopentadienyl derivatives.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 265  Similarly, the alkali metals react with [cyclooctatetraene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclooctatetraene "Cyclooctatetraene") in tetrahydrofuran to give alkali metal [cyclooctatetraenides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclooctatetraenide "Cyclooctatetraenide"); for example, [dipotassium cyclooctatetraenide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dipotassium_cyclooctatetraenide "Dipotassium cyclooctatetraenide") (K2C8H8) is an important precursor to many metal cyclooctatetraenyl derivatives, such as [uranocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranocene "Uranocene").[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 266  The large and very weakly polarising alkali metal cations can stabilise large, aromatic, polarisable radical anions, such as the dark-green [sodium naphthalenide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_naphthalenide "Sodium naphthalenide"), Na+\[C10H8•]−, a strong reducing agent.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 266 

## Representative reactions of alkali metals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Representative reactions of alkali metals")]

### Reaction with oxygen

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Reaction with oxygen")]

Upon reacting with oxygen, alkali metals form [oxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxide "Oxide"), [peroxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peroxide "Peroxide"), [superoxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superoxide "Superoxide") and [suboxides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suboxide "Suboxide"). However, the first three are more common. The table below[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-miessler-153) shows the types of compounds formed in reaction with oxygen. The compound in brackets represents the minor product of combustion.

|                                                          |           |              |                |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | --------- | ------------ | -------------- |
| **Alkali metal**                                         | **Oxide** | **Peroxide** | **Superoxide** |
| [Li](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium")    | Li2O      | (Li2O2)      |                |
| [Na](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium "Sodium")      | (Na2O)    | Na2O2        |                |
| [K](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium "Potassium") |           |              | KO2            |
| [Rb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium")  |           |              | RbO2           |
| [Cs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium "Caesium")    |           |              | CsO2           |

The alkali metal peroxides are ionic compounds that are unstable in water. The peroxide anion is weakly bound to the cation, and it is hydrolysed, forming stronger covalent bonds.

- Na2O2 + 2H2O → 2NaOH + H2O2

The other oxygen compounds are also unstable in water.

- - 2KO2 + 2H2O → 2KOH + H2O2 + O2[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154)
  - Li2O + H2O → 2LiOH

### Reaction with sulfur

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Reaction with sulfur")]

With sulfur, they form [sulfides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfide "Sulfide") and [polysulfides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polysulfide "Polysulfide").[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-155)

- 2Na + 1/8S8 → Na2S + 1/8S8 → Na2S2...Na2S7

Because alkali metal sulfides are essentially salts of a weak acid and a strong base, they form basic solutions.

- - S2- + H2O → HS− + HO−
  - HS− + H2O → H2S + HO−

### Reaction with nitrogen

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Reaction with nitrogen")]

Lithium is the only metal that combines directly with nitrogen at room temperature.

- 3Li + 1/2N2 → Li3N

Li3N can react with water to liberate ammonia.

- Li3N + 3H2O → 3LiOH + NH3

### Reaction with hydrogen

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Reaction with hydrogen")]

With hydrogen, alkali metals form saline [hydrides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydride "Hydride") that hydrolyse in water.

- - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {2 Na \ + H2 \ ->\[{\ce {\Delta}}\] \ 2 NaH}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/31df93df654a7b734a6adf394ab9bc5b69484a3a)
  - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {2NaH\ +\ 2H2O\ \longrightarrow \ 2NaOH\ +\ H2\uparrow }}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/87d6c6ce762a61f04e5efe1aa64cf8798337ea92)

### Reaction with carbon

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=35 "Edit section: Reaction with carbon")]

Lithium is the only metal that reacts directly with carbon to give [dilithium acetylide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilithium_acetylide "Dilithium acetylide"). Na and K can react with [acetylene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylene "Acetylene") to give [acetylides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylide "Acetylide").[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-156)

- - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {2Li\ +\ 2C\ \longrightarrow \ Li2C2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/852f130b88236a90d3c926228d364704d03eb37d)

  - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {2Na\ +\ 2C2H2\ ->\[{\ce {150\ ^{o}C}}\]\ 2NaC2H\ +\ H2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a763ecb3a7dd778b986b68bdebab4f5b5a28ce7a)

  - ![{\displaystyle {\ce {2Na\ +\ 2NaC2H\ ->\[{\ce {220\ ^{o}C}}\]\ 2Na2C2\ +\ H2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5dee10d9e2a8694d6b12badfe5ee74aa5e4d66c0)

### Reaction with water

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Reaction with water")]

On reaction with water, they generate [hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxide "Hydroxide") ions and [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen "Hydrogen") gas. This reaction is vigorous and highly exothermic and the hydrogen resulted may ignite in air or even explode in the case of Rb and Cs.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-miessler-153)

- Na + H2O → NaOH + 1/2H2

### Reaction with other salts

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=37 "Edit section: Reaction with other salts")]

The alkali metals are very good reducing agents. They can reduce metal cations that are less electropositive. [Titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium "Titanium") is produced industrially by the reduction of [titanium tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium_tetrachloride "Titanium tetrachloride") with Na at 400 °C ([van Arkel–de Boer process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Arkel%E2%80%93de_Boer_process "Van Arkel–de Boer process")).

- TiCl4 + 4Na → 4NaCl + Ti

### Reaction with organohalide compounds

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=38 "Edit section: Reaction with organohalide compounds")]

Alkali metals react with halogen derivatives to generate hydrocarbon via the [Wurtz reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wurtz_reaction "Wurtz reaction").

- 2CH3-Cl + 2Na → H3C-CH3 + 2NaCl

### Alkali metals in liquid ammonia

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=39 "Edit section: Alkali metals in liquid ammonia")]

Alkali metals dissolve in liquid [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia") or other donor solvents like aliphatic [amines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amine "Amine") or [hexamethylphosphoramide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hexamethylphosphoramide "Hexamethylphosphoramide") to give blue solutions. These solutions are believed to contain free electrons.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-miessler-153)

- Na + xNH3 → Na+ + e(NH3)x−

Due to the presence of [solvated electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solvated_electron "Solvated electron"), these solutions are very powerful reducing agents used in organic synthesis.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Na_in_lie._am.jpg/280px-Na_in_lie._am.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Na_in_lie._am.jpg)

Reduction reactions using sodium in liquid ammonia

Reaction 1) is known as [Birch reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birch_reduction "Birch reduction"). Other reductions[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-miessler-153) that can be carried by these solutions are:

- - S8 + 2e− → S82-
  - Fe(CO)5 + 2e− → Fe(CO)42- + CO

## Extensions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=40 "Edit section: Extensions")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Atomic_radius_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg/250px-Atomic_radius_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Atomic_radius_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg)

[Empirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") (Na–Cs, Mg–Ra) and predicted (Fr–Uhp, Ubn–Uhh) atomic radius of the alkali and alkaline earth metals from the [third](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_3_element "Period 3 element") to the [ninth period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_9_element "Period 9 element"), measured in [angstroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angstrom "Angstrom")[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1730 [\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pyykko-157)

Although francium is the heaviest alkali metal that has been discovered, there has been some theoretical work predicting the physical and chemical characteristics of hypothetical heavier alkali metals. Being the first [period 8 element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_8_element "Period 8 element"), the undiscovered element [ununennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ununennium "Ununennium") (element 119) is predicted to be the next alkali metal after francium and behave much like their lighter [congeners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congener_\(chemistry\) "Congener (chemistry)"); however, it is also predicted to differ from the lighter alkali metals in some properties.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  Its chemistry is predicted to be closer to that of potassium[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-EB-47) or rubidium[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  instead of caesium or francium. This is unusual as [periodic trends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_trends "Periodic trends"), ignoring relativistic effects would predict ununennium to be even more reactive than caesium and francium. This lowered [reactivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactivity_\(chemistry\) "Reactivity (chemistry)") is due to the relativistic stabilisation of ununennium's valence electron, increasing ununennium's first ionisation energy and decreasing the [metallic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_radius "Metallic radius") and [ionic radii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_radii "Ionic radii");[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-EB-47) this effect is already seen for francium.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  This assumes that ununennium will behave chemically as an alkali metal, which, although likely, may not be true due to relativistic effects.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tanm-158) The relativistic stabilisation of the 8s orbital also increases ununennium's [electron affinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_affinity "Electron affinity") far beyond that of caesium and francium; indeed, ununennium is expected to have an electron affinity higher than all the alkali metals lighter than it. Relativistic effects also cause a very large drop in the [polarisability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarisability "Polarisability") of ununennium.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  On the other hand, ununennium is predicted to continue the trend of melting points decreasing going down the group, being expected to have a melting point between 0 °C and 30 °C.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1724 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Electron_affinity_of_alkali_metals.svg/220px-Electron_affinity_of_alkali_metals.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Electron_affinity_of_alkali_metals.svg)

Empirical (Na–Fr) and predicted (Uue) electron affinity of the alkali metals from the third to the [eighth period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_8_element "Period 8 element"), measured in [electron volts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_volt "Electron volt")[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1730 [\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pyykko-157)

The stabilisation of ununennium's valence electron and thus the contraction of the 8s orbital cause its atomic radius to be lowered to 240 [pm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picometer "Picometer"),[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  very close to that of rubidium (247 pm),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) so that the chemistry of ununennium in the +1 oxidation state should be more similar to the chemistry of rubidium than to that of francium. On the other hand, the ionic radius of the Uue+ ion is predicted to be larger than that of Rb+, because the 7p orbitals are destabilised and are thus larger than the p-orbitals of the lower shells. Ununennium may also show the +3[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  and +5 [oxidation states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidation_state "Oxidation state"),[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159) which are not seen in any other alkali metal,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  in addition to the +1 oxidation state that is characteristic of the other alkali metals and is also the main oxidation state of all the known alkali metals: this is because of the destabilisation and expansion of the 7p3/2 spinor, causing its outermost electrons to have a lower ionisation energy than what would otherwise be expected.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28 [\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  Indeed, many ununennium compounds are expected to have a large [covalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent "Covalent") character, due to the involvement of the 7p3/2 electrons in the bonding.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Thayer-96)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Ionization_energy_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg/220px-Ionization_energy_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ionization_energy_of_alkali_metals_and_alkaline_earth_metals.svg)

Empirical (Na–Fr, Mg–Ra) and predicted (Uue–Uhp, Ubn–Uhh) ionisation energy of the alkali and alkaline earth metals from the third to the ninth period, measured in electron volts[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1730 [\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pyykko-157)

Not as much work has been done predicting the properties of the alkali metals beyond ununennium. Although a simple extrapolation of the periodic table (by the [Aufbau principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aufbau_principle "Aufbau principle")) would put element 169, unhexennium, under ununennium, Dirac-Fock calculations predict that the next element after ununennium with alkali-metal-like properties may be element 165, unhexpentium, which is predicted to have the electron configuration \[Og] 5g18 6f14 7d10 8s2 8p1/22 9s1.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730 [\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pyykko-157) This element would be intermediate in properties between an alkali metal and a [group 11 element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_11_element "Group 11 element"), and while its physical and atomic properties would be closer to the former, its chemistry may be closer to that of the latter. Further calculations show that unhexpentium would follow the trend of increasing ionisation energy beyond caesium, having an ionisation energy comparable to that of sodium, and that it should also continue the trend of decreasing atomic radii beyond caesium, having an atomic radius comparable to that of potassium.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  However, the 7d electrons of unhexpentium may also be able to participate in chemical reactions along with the 9s electron, possibly allowing oxidation states beyond +1, whence the likely transition metal behaviour of unhexpentium.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1732–1733 [\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-BFricke-160) Due to the alkali and [alkaline earth metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_earth_metal "Alkaline earth metal") both being [s-block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-block "S-block") elements, these predictions for the trends and properties of ununennium and unhexpentium also mostly hold quite similarly for the corresponding alkaline earth metals [unbinilium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unbinilium "Unbinilium") (Ubn) and unhexhexium (Uhh).[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1733  Unsepttrium, element 173, may be an even better heavier homologue of ununennium; with a predicted electron configuration of \[Usb] 6g1, it returns to the alkali-metal-like situation of having one easily removed electron far above a closed p-shell in energy, and is expected to be even more reactive than caesium.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-BFricke1977-161)[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-primefan-162)

The probable properties of further alkali metals beyond unsepttrium have not been explored yet as of 2019, and they may or may not be able to exist.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pyykko-157) In periods 8 and above of the periodic table, relativistic and shell-structure effects become so strong that extrapolations from lighter congeners become completely inaccurate.
