In addition, the relativistic and shell-structure effects (which stabilise the s-orbitals and destabilise and expand the d-, f-, and g-orbitals of higher shells) have opposite effects, causing even larger difference between relativistic and non-relativistic calculations of the properties of elements with such high atomic numbers.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1732–1733  Interest in the chemical properties of ununennium, unhexpentium, and unsepttrium stems from the fact that they are located close to the expected locations of [islands of stability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_of_stability "Island of stability"), centered at elements 122 (306Ubb) and 164 (482Uhq).[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Kratz-163)[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-164)[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165)

## Pseudo-alkali metals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=41 "Edit section: Pseudo-alkali metals")]

Many other substances are similar to the alkali metals in their tendency to form monopositive cations. Analogously to the [pseudohalogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudohalogen "Pseudohalogen"), they have sometimes been called "pseudo-alkali metals". These substances include some elements and many more [polyatomic ions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyatomic_ion "Polyatomic ion"); the polyatomic ions are especially similar to the alkali metals in their large size and weak polarising power.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pseudo-166)

### Hydrogen

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=42 "Edit section: Hydrogen")]

The element [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen "Hydrogen"), with one electron per neutral atom, is usually placed at the top of Group 1 of the periodic table because of its electron configuration. But hydrogen is not normally considered to be an alkali metal.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-iupac-167) [Metallic hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_hydrogen "Metallic hydrogen"), which only exists at very high pressures, is known for its electrical and magnetic properties, not its chemical properties.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Folden-168) Under typical conditions, pure hydrogen exists as a [diatomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diatomic "Diatomic") gas consisting of two atoms per molecule (H2);[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-169) however, the alkali metals form diatomic molecules (such as [dilithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilithium "Dilithium"), Li2) only at high temperatures, when they are in the gaseous state.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-170)

Hydrogen, like the alkali metals, has one [valence electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valence_electron "Valence electron")[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132) and reacts easily with the [halogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halogen "Halogen"),[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132) but the similarities mostly end there because of the small size of a bare proton H+ compared to the alkali metal cations.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132) Its placement above lithium is primarily due to its [electron configuration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_configuration "Electron configuration").[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-iupac-167) It is sometimes placed above [fluorine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorine "Fluorine") due to their similar chemical properties, though the resemblance is likewise not absolute.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hydrogen-171)

The first ionisation energy of hydrogen (1312.0 [kJ/mol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KJ/mol "KJ/mol")) is much higher than that of the alkali metals.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-huheey-172)[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-macmillan-173) As only one additional electron is required to fill in the outermost shell of the hydrogen atom, hydrogen often behaves like a halogen, forming the negative [hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydride "Hydride") ion, and is very occasionally considered to be a halogen on that basis. (The alkali metals can also form negative ions, known as [alkalides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkalide "Alkalide"), but these are little more than laboratory curiosities, being unstable.)[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HNa-89)[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-HNa-theory-90) An argument against this placement is that formation of hydride from hydrogen is endothermic, unlike the exothermic formation of halides from halogens. The radius of the H− anion also does not fit the trend of increasing size going down the halogens: indeed, H− is very diffuse because its single proton cannot easily control both electrons.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 15–6  It was expected for some time that liquid hydrogen would show metallic properties;[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hydrogen-171) while this has been shown to not be the case, under extremely high [pressures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pressure "Pressure"), such as those found at the cores of [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter "Jupiter") and [Saturn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn "Saturn"), hydrogen does become metallic and behaves like an alkali metal; in this phase, it is known as [metallic hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_hydrogen "Metallic hydrogen").[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-174) The [electrical resistivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_resistivity "Electrical resistivity") of liquid [metallic hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_hydrogen "Metallic hydrogen") at 3000 K is approximately equal to that of liquid [rubidium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium") and [caesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium "Caesium") at 2000 K at the respective pressures when they undergo a nonmetal-to-metal transition.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-175)

The 1s1 electron configuration of hydrogen, while analogous to that of the alkali metals (ns1), is unique because there is no 1p subshell. Hence it can lose an electron to form the [hydron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydron_\(chemistry\) "Hydron (chemistry)") H+, or gain one to form the [hydride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydride "Hydride") ion H−.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 43  In the former case it resembles superficially the alkali metals; in the latter case, the halogens, but the differences due to the lack of a 1p subshell are important enough that neither group fits the properties of hydrogen well.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 43  Group 14 is also a good fit in terms of thermodynamic properties such as [ionisation energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionisation_energy "Ionisation energy") and [electron affinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_affinity "Electron affinity"), but hydrogen cannot be tetravalent. Thus none of the three placements are entirely satisfactory, although group 1 is the most common placement (if one is chosen) because of the electron configuration and the fact that the hydron is by far the most important of all monatomic hydrogen species, being the foundation of acid-base chemistry.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hydrogen-171) As an example of hydrogen's unorthodox properties stemming from its unusual electron configuration and small size, the hydrogen ion is very small (radius around 150 [fm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femtometre "Femtometre") compared to the 50–220 pm size of most other atoms and ions) and so is nonexistent in condensed systems other than in association with other atoms or molecules. Indeed, transferring of protons between chemicals is the basis of [acid-base chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid-base_chemistry "Acid-base chemistry").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 43  Also unique is hydrogen's ability to form [hydrogen bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_bond "Hydrogen bond"), which are an effect of charge-transfer, [electrostatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrostatic "Electrostatic"), and electron correlative contributing phenomena.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hydrogen-171) While analogous lithium bonds are also known, they are mostly electrostatic.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hydrogen-171) Nevertheless, hydrogen can take on the same structural role as the alkali metals in some molecular crystals, and has a close relationship with the lightest alkali metals (especially lithium).[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-176)

### Ammonium and derivatives

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=43 "Edit section: Ammonium and derivatives")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Hydrochloric_acid_ammonia.jpg/220px-Hydrochloric_acid_ammonia.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hydrochloric_acid_ammonia.jpg)

Similarly to the alkali metals, [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia") reacts with [hydrochloric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid "Hydrochloric acid") to form the salt [ammonium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonium_chloride "Ammonium chloride").

The [ammonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonium "Ammonium") ion (NH+4) has very similar properties to the heavier alkali metals, acting as an alkali metal intermediate between potassium and rubidium,[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pseudo-166)[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-177)[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-178) and is often considered a close relative.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Holleman\&Wiberg-179)[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Stevenson-180)[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bernal\&Massey-181) For example, most alkali metal [salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salts "Salts") are [soluble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soluble "Soluble") in water, a property which ammonium salts share.[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-182) Ammonium is expected to behave stably as a metal (NH+4 ions in a sea of delocalised electrons) at very high pressures (though less than the typical pressure where transitions from insulating to metallic behaviour occur around, 100 [GPa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pascal_\(unit\) "Pascal (unit)")), and could possibly occur inside the [ice giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_giant "Ice giant") [Uranus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranus "Uranus") and [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune "Neptune"), which may have significant impacts on their interior magnetic fields.[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Stevenson-180)[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bernal\&Massey-181) It has been estimated that the transition from a mixture of [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia") and dihydrogen molecules to metallic ammonium may occur at pressures just below 25 GPa.[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Stevenson-180) Under standard conditions, ammonium can form a metallic amalgam with mercury.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-183)

Other "pseudo-alkali metals" include the [alkylammonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkylammonium "Alkylammonium") cations, in which some of the hydrogen atoms in the ammonium cation are replaced by alkyl or aryl groups. In particular, the [quaternary ammonium cations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_ammonium_cation "Quaternary ammonium cation") (NR+4) are very useful since they are permanently charged, and they are often used as an alternative to the expensive Cs+ to stabilise very large and very easily polarisable anions such as HI−2.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 812–9  Tetraalkylammonium hydroxides, like alkali metal hydroxides, are very strong bases that react with atmospheric carbon dioxide to form carbonates.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 256  Furthermore, the nitrogen atom may be replaced by a phosphorus, arsenic, or antimony atom (the heavier nonmetallic [pnictogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pnictogen "Pnictogen")), creating a [phosphonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphonium "Phosphonium") (PH+4) or [arsonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsonium "Arsonium") (AsH+4) cation that can itself be substituted similarly; while [stibonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Stibonium\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Stibonium (page does not exist)") (SbH+4) itself is not known, some of its organic derivatives are characterised.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pseudo-166)

### Cobaltocene and derivatives

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=44 "Edit section: Cobaltocene and derivatives")]

[Cobaltocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobaltocene "Cobaltocene"), Co(C5H5)2, is a [metallocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallocene "Metallocene"), the [cobalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobalt "Cobalt") analogue of [ferrocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrocene "Ferrocene"). It is a dark purple solid. Cobaltocene has 19 valence electrons, one more than usually found in organotransition metal complexes, such as its very stable relative, ferrocene, in accordance with the [18-electron rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18-electron_rule "18-electron rule"). This additional electron occupies an orbital that is antibonding with respect to the Co–C bonds. Consequently, many chemical reactions of Co(C5H5)2 are characterized by its tendency to lose this "extra" electron, yielding a very stable 18-electron cation known as cobaltocenium. Many cobaltocenium salts coprecipitate with caesium salts, and cobaltocenium hydroxide is a strong base that absorbs atmospheric carbon dioxide to form cobaltocenium carbonate.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 256  Like the alkali metals, cobaltocene is a strong reducing agent, and [decamethylcobaltocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decamethylcobaltocene "Decamethylcobaltocene") is stronger still due to the combined [inductive effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_effect "Inductive effect") of the ten methyl groups.[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-184) Cobalt may be substituted by its heavier congener [rhodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodium "Rhodium") to give [rhodocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodocene "Rhodocene"), an even stronger reducing agent.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-185) [Iridocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridocene "Iridocene") (involving [iridium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium "Iridium")) would presumably be still more potent, but is not very well-studied due to its instability.[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Keller_1967-186)

### Thallium

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=45 "Edit section: Thallium")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Thallium_pieces_in_ampoule.jpg/220px-Thallium_pieces_in_ampoule.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Thallium_pieces_in_ampoule.jpg)

Very pure thallium pieces in a glass [ampoule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ampoule "Ampoule"), stored under [argon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon "Argon") gas

[Thallium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thallium "Thallium") is the heaviest stable element in group 13 of the periodic table. At the bottom of the periodic table, the [inert-pair effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inert-pair_effect "Inert-pair effect") is quite strong, because of the [relativistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_effects "Relativistic effects") stabilisation of the 6s orbital and the decreasing bond energy as the atoms increase in size so that the amount of energy released in forming two more bonds is not worth the high ionisation energies of the 6s electrons.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 226–7  It displays the +1 [oxidation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidation_state "Oxidation state")[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  that all the known alkali metals display,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  and thallium compounds with thallium in its +1 [oxidation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidation_state "Oxidation state") closely resemble the corresponding potassium or silver compounds stoichiometrically due to the similar ionic radii of the Tl+ (164 [pm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picometer "Picometer")), K+ (152 pm) and Ag+ (129 pm) ions.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Shannon-187)[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Crookes-188) It was sometimes considered an alkali metal in [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe "Continental Europe") (but not in England) in the years immediately following its discovery,[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Crookes-188): 126  and was placed just after caesium as the sixth alkali metal in [Dmitri Mendeleev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitri_Mendeleev "Dmitri Mendeleev")'s 1869 [periodic table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Periodic_table "Periodic table") and [Julius Lothar Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Lothar_Meyer "Julius Lothar Meyer")'s 1868 periodic table.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-meta-synthesis2-25) Mendeleev's 1871 periodic table and Meyer's 1870 periodic table put thallium in its current position in the [boron group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron_group "Boron group") and left the space below caesium blank.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-meta-synthesis2-25) However, thallium also displays the oxidation state +3,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  which no known alkali metal displays[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 28  (although ununennium, the undiscovered seventh alkali metal, is predicted to possibly display the +3 oxidation state).[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Uue-41): 1729–1730  The sixth alkali metal is now considered to be francium.[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-redbook-189) While Tl+ is stabilised by the inert-pair effect, this inert pair of 6s electrons is still able to participate chemically, so that these electrons are [stereochemically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereochemistry "Stereochemistry") active in aqueous solution. Additionally, the thallium halides (except [TlF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TlF "TlF")) are quite insoluble in water, and [TlI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TlI "TlI") has an unusual structure because of the presence of the stereochemically active inert pair in thallium.[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-190)

### Copper, silver, and gold

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=46 "Edit section: Copper, silver, and gold")]

[![A crystal of a coppery-colored metal mineral of standing on a white surface](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/NatCopper.jpg/110px-NatCopper.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NatCopper.jpg)

Copper

[![A crystal of a silvery metal crystal lying on a grey surface](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Silver_crystal.jpg/132px-Silver_crystal.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Silver_crystal.jpg)

Silver

[![A crystal of a yellow metal lying on a white surface](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Gold-crystals.jpg/160px-Gold-crystals.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gold-crystals.jpg)

Gold

The [group 11 metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_11_element "Group 11 element") (or coinage metals), copper, silver, and gold, are typically categorised as transition metals given they can form ions with incomplete d-shells. Physically, they have the relatively low melting points and high electronegativity values associated with [post-transition metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-transition_metal "Post-transition metal"). "The filled *d* subshell and free *s* electron of Cu, Ag, and Au contribute to their high electrical and thermal conductivity. Transition metals to the left of group 11 experience interactions between *s* electrons and the partially filled *d* subshell that lower electron mobility."[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-191) Chemically, the group 11 metals behave like main-group metals in their +1 valence states, and are hence somewhat related to the alkali metals: this is one reason for their previously being labelled as "group IB", paralleling the alkali metals' "group IA". They are occasionally classified as post-transition metals.[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-192) Their spectra are analogous to those of the alkali metals.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jensen-33) Their monopositive ions are [paramagnetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramagnetic "Paramagnetic") and contribute no colour to their salts, like those of the alkali metals.[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-193)

In Mendeleev's 1871 periodic table, copper, silver, and gold are listed twice, once under group VIII (with the [iron triad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_triad "Iron triad") and [platinum group metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platinum_group_metal "Platinum group metal")), and once under group IB. Group IB was nonetheless parenthesised to note that it was tentative. Mendeleev's main criterion for group assignment was the maximum oxidation state of an element: on that basis, the group 11 elements could not be classified in group IB, due to the existence of copper(II) and gold(III) compounds being known at that time.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jensen-33) However, eliminating group IB would make group I the only main group (group VIII was labelled a transition group) to lack an A–B bifurcation.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jensen-33) Soon afterward, a majority of chemists chose to classify these elements in group IB and remove them from group VIII for the resulting symmetry: this was the predominant classification until the rise of the modern medium-long 18-column periodic table, which separated the alkali metals and group 11 metals.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jensen-33)

The coinage metals were traditionally regarded as a subdivision of the alkali metal group, due to them sharing the characteristic s1 electron configuration of the alkali metals (group 1: p6s1; group 11: d10s1). However, the similarities are largely confined to the [stoichiometries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoichiometries "Stoichiometries") of the +1 compounds of both groups, and not their chemical properties.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177  This stems from the filled d subshell providing a much weaker shielding effect on the outermost s electron than the filled p subshell, so that the coinage metals have much higher first ionisation energies and smaller ionic radii than do the corresponding alkali metals.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177  Furthermore, they have higher melting points, hardnesses, and densities, and lower reactivities and solubilities in liquid [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia"), as well as having more covalent character in their compounds.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177  Finally, the alkali metals are at the top of the [electrochemical series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrochemical_series "Electrochemical series"), whereas the coinage metals are almost at the very bottom.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177  The coinage metals' filled d shell is much more easily disrupted than the alkali metals' filled p shell, so that the second and third ionisation energies are lower, enabling higher oxidation states than +1 and a richer coordination chemistry, thus giving the group 11 metals clear [transition metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_metal "Transition metal") character.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177  Particularly noteworthy is gold forming ionic compounds with rubidium and caesium, in which it forms the auride ion (Au−) which also occurs in solvated form in liquid ammonia solution: here gold behaves as a [pseudohalogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudohalogen "Pseudohalogen") because its 5d106s1 configuration has one electron less than the quasi-closed shell 5d106s2 configuration of [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\) "Mercury (element)").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 1177 

## Production and isolation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=47 "Edit section: Production and isolation")]

[![alt1](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Lithium_mine%2C_Salar_del_Hombre_Muerto%2C_Argentina.jpg/160px-Lithium_mine%2C_Salar_del_Hombre_Muerto%2C_Argentina.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lithium_mine,_Salar_del_Hombre_Muerto,_Argentina.jpg)

[![alt2](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Uyuni_landsat.JPG/105px-Uyuni_landsat.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Uyuni_landsat.JPG)

[Salt flats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_pan_\(geology\) "Salt pan (geology)") are rich in lithium, such as these in Salar del Hombre Muerto, Argentina (left) and [Uyuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salar_de_Uyuni "Salar de Uyuni"), Bolivia (right). The lithium-rich brine is concentrated by pumping it into [solar evaporation ponds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_evaporation_pond "Salt evaporation pond") (visible in Argentina image).

The production of pure alkali metals is somewhat complicated due to their extreme reactivity with commonly used substances, such as water.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9)[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) From their [silicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicate "Silicate") ores, all the stable alkali metals may be obtained the same way: [sulfuric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfuric_acid "Sulfuric acid") is first used to dissolve the desired alkali metal ion and aluminium(III) ions from the ore (leaching), whereupon basic precipitation removes aluminium ions from the mixture by precipitating it as the [hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium_hydroxide "Aluminium hydroxide"). The remaining insoluble alkali metal [carbonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonate "Carbonate") is then precipitated selectively; the salt is then dissolved in [hydrochloric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid "Hydrochloric acid") to produce the chloride. The result is then left to evaporate and the alkali metal can then be isolated.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Lithium and sodium are typically isolated through electrolysis from their liquid chlorides, with [calcium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium_chloride "Calcium chloride") typically added to lower the melting point of the mixture. The heavier alkali metals, however, are more typically isolated in a different way, where a reducing agent (typically sodium for potassium and [magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium "Magnesium") or [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium") for the heaviest alkali metals) is used to reduce the alkali metal chloride. The liquid or gaseous product (the alkali metal) then undergoes [fractional distillation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractional_distillation "Fractional distillation") for purification.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-generalchemistry-71) Most routes to the pure alkali metals require the use of electrolysis due to their high reactivity; one of the few which does not is the [pyrolysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrolysis "Pyrolysis") of the corresponding alkali metal [azide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azide "Azide"), which yields the metal for sodium, potassium, rubidium, and caesium and the nitride for lithium.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-King-132): 77 

Lithium salts have to be extracted from the water of [mineral springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_spring "Mineral spring"), [brine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brine "Brine") pools, and brine deposits. The metal is produced electrolytically from a mixture of fused [lithium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_chloride "Lithium chloride") and [potassium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_chloride "Potassium chloride").[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ober-194)

Sodium occurs mostly in seawater and dried [seabed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seabed "Seabed"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) but is now produced through [electrolysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrolysis "Electrolysis") of [sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride") by lowering the melting point of the substance to below 700 °C through the use of a [Downs cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downs_cell "Downs cell").[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pauling-195)[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-losal-196) Extremely pure sodium can be produced through the thermal decomposition of [sodium azide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_azide "Sodium azide").[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-197) Potassium occurs in many minerals, such as [sylvite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvite "Sylvite") ([potassium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_chloride "Potassium chloride")).[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) Previously, potassium was generally made from the electrolysis of [potassium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_chloride "Potassium chloride") or [potassium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_hydroxide "Potassium hydroxide"),[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-198) found extensively in places such as Canada, Russia, Belarus, Germany, Israel, United States, and Jordan, in a method similar to how sodium was produced in the late 1800s and early 1900s.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-kirk-199) It can also be produced from [seawater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seawater "Seawater").[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-rsc-9) However, these methods are problematic because the potassium metal tends to dissolve in its molten chloride and vaporises significantly at the operating temperatures, potentially forming the explosive superoxide. As a result, pure potassium metal is now produced by reducing molten potassium chloride with sodium metal at 850 °C.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74 

- Na (g) + KCl (l) ⇌ NaCl (l) + K (g)

Although sodium is less reactive than potassium, this process works because at such high temperatures potassium is more volatile than sodium and can easily be distilled off, so that the equilibrium shifts towards the right to produce more potassium gas and proceeds almost to completion.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74 

Metals like sodium are obtained by electrolysis of molten salts. Rb & Cs obtained mainly as by products of Li processing. To make pure caesium, ores of caesium and rubidium are crushed and heated to 650 °C with sodium metal, generating an alloy that can then be separated via a [fractional distillation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractional_distillation "Fractional distillation") technique. Because metallic caesium is too reactive to handle, it is normally offered as [caesium azide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium_azide "Caesium azide") (CsN3). [Caesium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium_hydroxide "Caesium hydroxide") is formed when caesium interacts aggressively with water and ice (CsOH).[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-200)

[Rubidium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium") is the 16th most abundant element in the earth's crust; however, it is quite rare. Some minerals found in North America, South Africa, Russia, and Canada contain rubidium. Some potassium minerals ([lepidolites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite"), [biotites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biotite "Biotite"), [feldspar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feldspar "Feldspar"), [carnallite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnallite "Carnallite")) contain it, together with caesium. [Pollucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollucite "Pollucite"), [carnallite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnallite "Carnallite"), [leucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leucite "Leucite"), and [lepidolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite") are all minerals that contain rubidium. As a by-product of lithium extraction, it is commercially obtained from [lepidolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepidolite "Lepidolite"). Rubidium is also found in potassium rocks and [brines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brine "Brine"), which is a commercial supply. The majority of rubidium is now obtained as a byproduct of refining lithium. Rubidium is used in [vacuum tubes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum_tube "Vacuum tube") as a [getter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getter "Getter"), a material that combines with and removes trace gases from vacuum tubes.[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-201)[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-202)

[![A shiny gray 5-centimeter piece of matter with a rough surface.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Pichblende.jpg/220px-Pichblende.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pichblende.jpg)

This sample of [uraninite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uraninite "Uraninite") contains about 100,000 atoms (3.3×10−20 g) of francium-223 at any given time.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nbb-66)

For several years in the 1950s and 1960s, a by-product of the potassium production called Alkarb was a main source for rubidium. Alkarb contained 21% rubidium while the rest was potassium and a small fraction of caesium.[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-203) Today the largest producers of caesium, for example the [Tanco Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanco_Mine "Tanco Mine") in Manitoba, Canada, produce rubidium as by-product from [pollucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollucite "Pollucite").[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USGS-204) Today, a common method for separating rubidium from potassium and caesium is the [fractional crystallisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fractional_crystallization_\(chemistry\) "Fractional crystallization (chemistry)") of a rubidium and caesium [alum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alum "Alum") ([Cs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium "Caesium"), [Rb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium "Rubidium"))[Al](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium "Aluminium")([SO4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfate "Sulfate"))2·12[H2O](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water "Water"), which yields pure rubidium alum after approximately 30 recrystallisations.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USGS-204)[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-205) The limited applications and the lack of a mineral rich in rubidium limit the production of rubidium compounds to 2 to 4 [tonnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonne "Tonne") per year.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USGS-204) Caesium, however, is not produced from the above reaction. Instead, the mining of [pollucite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollucite "Pollucite") ore is the main method of obtaining pure caesium, extracted from the ore mainly by three methods: acid digestion, alkaline decomposition, and direct reduction.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USGS-204)[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Burt-206) Both metals are produced as by-products of lithium production: after 1958, when interest in lithium's thermonuclear properties increased sharply, the production of rubidium and caesium also increased correspondingly.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 71  Pure rubidium and caesium metals are produced by reducing their chlorides with [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium "Calcium") metal at 750 °C and low pressure.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74 

As a result of its extreme rarity in nature,[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Winter-68) most francium is synthesised in the nuclear reaction 197[Au](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold "Gold") + 18[O](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen "Oxygen") → 210[Fr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium "Francium") + 5 [n](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron "Neutron"), yielding [francium-209](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium-209 "Francium-209"), [francium-210](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium-210 "Francium-210"), and [francium-211](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francium-211 "Francium-211").[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-207) The greatest quantity of francium ever assembled to date is about 300,000 neutral atoms,[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemnews-208) which were synthesised using the nuclear reaction given above.[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-chemnews-208) When the only natural isotope francium-223 is specifically required, it is produced as the alpha daughter of actinium-227, itself produced synthetically from the neutron irradiation of natural radium-226, one of the daughters of natural uranium-238.[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-andyscouse-209)

## Applications

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=48 "Edit section: Applications")]

Lithium, sodium, and potassium have many useful applications, while rubidium and caesium are very notable in academic contexts but do not have many applications yet.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 68  Lithium is the key ingredient for a [range of lithium-based batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_battery "Lithium battery"), and [lithium oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_oxide "Lithium oxide") can help process silica. [Lithium stearate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_stearate "Lithium stearate") is a thickener and can be used to make lubricating greases; it is produced from lithium hydroxide, which is also used to absorb [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide "Carbon dioxide") in space capsules and submarines.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 70  [Lithium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_chloride "Lithium chloride") is used as a brazing alloy for aluminium parts.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-210) In medicine, some [lithium salts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_\(medication\) "Lithium (medication)") are used as mood-stabilising pharmaceuticals. Metallic lithium is used in alloys with magnesium and aluminium to give very tough and light alloys.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 70 

Sodium compounds have many applications, the most well-known being sodium chloride as [table salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_salt "Table salt"). Sodium salts of [fatty acids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatty_acid "Fatty acid") are used as soap.[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-211) Pure sodium metal also has many applications, including use in [sodium-vapour lamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium-vapor_lamp "Sodium-vapor lamp"), which produce very efficient light compared to other types of lighting,[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-lamp1-212)[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-lamp2-213) and can help smooth the surface of other metals.[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-214)[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-215) Being a strong reducing agent, it is often used to reduce many other metals, such as [titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium "Titanium") and [zirconium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirconium "Zirconium"), from their chlorides. Furthermore, it is very useful as a heat-exchange liquid in [fast breeder nuclear reactors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_breeder_nuclear_reactor "Fast breeder nuclear reactor") due to its low melting point, viscosity, and [cross-section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-section_\(physics\) "Cross-section (physics)") towards neutron absorption.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74  [Sodium-ion batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium-ion_batteries "Sodium-ion batteries") may provide cheaper alternatives to their equivalent lithium-based cells. Both sodium and potassium are commonly used as [GRAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRAS "GRAS") counterions to create more water-soluble and hence more bioavailable salt forms of acidic pharmaceuticals.[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-216)

Potassium compounds are often used as [fertilisers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertiliser "Fertiliser")[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 73 [\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-217) as potassium is an important element for plant nutrition. [Potassium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_hydroxide "Potassium hydroxide") is a very strong base, and is used to control the [pH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PH "PH") of various substances.[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-218)[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-219) [Potassium nitrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_nitrate "Potassium nitrate") and [potassium permanganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_permanganate "Potassium permanganate") are often used as powerful oxidising agents.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 73  [Potassium superoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_superoxide "Potassium superoxide") is used in breathing masks, as it reacts with carbon dioxide to give potassium carbonate and oxygen gas. Pure potassium metal is not often used, but its alloys with sodium may substitute for pure sodium in fast breeder nuclear reactors.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Greenwood\&Earnshaw-14): 74 

Rubidium and caesium are often used in [atomic clocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_clock "Atomic clock").[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomic-clocks-220) Caesium atomic clocks are extraordinarily accurate; if a clock had been made at the time of the dinosaurs, it would be off by less than four seconds (after 80 million years).[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pubs.usgs-62) For that reason, caesium atoms are used as the definition of the second.[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nist-second-221) Rubidium ions are often used in purple [fireworks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firework "Firework"),[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-222) and caesium is often used in drilling fluids in the petroleum industry.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pubs.usgs-62)[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-223)

Francium has no commercial applications,[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nbb-66)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-elemental-67)[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-224) but because of francium's relatively simple [atomic structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_structure "Atomic structure"), among other things, it has been used in [spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectroscopy "Spectroscopy") experiments, leading to more information regarding [energy levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_level "Energy level") and the [coupling constants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coupling_constant "Coupling constant") between [subatomic particles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subatomic_particle "Subatomic particle").[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-225) Studies on the light emitted by laser-trapped francium-210 ions have provided accurate data on transitions between atomic energy levels, similar to those predicted by [quantum theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_mechanics "Quantum mechanics").[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-226)

## Biological role and precautions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=49 "Edit section: Biological role and precautions")]

### Metals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=50 "Edit section: Metals")]

Pure alkali metals are dangerously reactive with air and water and must be kept away from heat, fire, oxidising agents, acids, most organic compounds, [halocarbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halocarbon "Halocarbon"), plastics, and moisture. They also react with carbon dioxide and carbon tetrachloride, so that normal fire extinguishers are counterproductive when used on alkali metal fires.[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-osu-227) Some Class D dry powder [extinguishers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_extinguisher "Fire extinguisher") designed for metal fires are effective, depriving the fire of oxygen and cooling the alkali metal.[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-228)

Experiments are usually conducted using only small quantities of a few grams in a [fume hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fume_hood "Fume hood"). Small quantities of lithium may be disposed of by reaction with cool water, but the heavier alkali metals should be dissolved in the less reactive [isopropanol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isopropanol "Isopropanol").[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-osu-227)[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-229) The alkali metals must be stored under [mineral oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_oil "Mineral oil") or an inert atmosphere. The inert atmosphere used may be [argon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon "Argon") or nitrogen gas, except for lithium, which reacts with nitrogen.[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-osu-227) Rubidium and caesium must be kept away from air, even under oil, because even a small amount of air diffused into the oil may trigger formation of the dangerously explosive peroxide; for the same reason, potassium should not be stored under oil in an oxygen-containing atmosphere for longer than 6 months.[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-230)[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-231)

### Ions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=51 "Edit section: Ions")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Lithium_carbonate.jpg/220px-Lithium_carbonate.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lithium_carbonate.jpg)

[Lithium carbonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_carbonate "Lithium carbonate")

The bioinorganic chemistry of the alkali metal ions has been extensively reviewed.[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-232) Solid state crystal structures have been determined for many complexes of alkali metal ions in small peptides, nucleic acid constituents, carbohydrates and ionophore complexes.[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-233)

Lithium naturally only occurs in traces in biological systems and has no known biological role, but does have effects on the body when ingested.[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-lithium-234) [Lithium carbonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_carbonate "Lithium carbonate") is used as a [mood stabiliser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mood_stabiliser "Mood stabiliser") in [psychiatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychiatry "Psychiatry") to treat [bipolar disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipolar_disorder "Bipolar disorder") ([manic-depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manic-depression "Manic-depression")) in daily doses of about 0.5 to 2 grams, although there are side-effects.[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-lithium-234) Excessive ingestion of lithium causes drowsiness, slurred speech and vomiting, among other symptoms,[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-lithium-234) and [poisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poison "Poison") the [central nervous system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_nervous_system "Central nervous system"),[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-lithium-234) which is dangerous as the required dosage of lithium to treat bipolar disorder is only slightly lower than the toxic dosage.[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-lithium-234)[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-theodoregray-lithium-235) Its biochemistry, the way it is handled by the human body and studies using rats and goats suggest that it is an [essential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_element "Essential element") [trace element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trace_element "Trace element"), although the natural biological function of lithium in humans has yet to be identified.[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-236)[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-237)

Sodium and potassium occur in all known biological systems, generally functioning as [electrolytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrolytes "Electrolytes") inside and outside [cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_\(biology\) "Cell (biology)").[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-potassium-238)[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-sodium-239) Sodium is an essential nutrient that regulates blood volume, blood pressure, osmotic equilibrium and [pH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PH "PH"); the minimum physiological requirement for sodium is 500 milligrams per day.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-r31-240) [Sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride") (also known as common salt) is the principal source of sodium in the diet, and is used as seasoning and preservative, such as for [pickling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickling "Pickling") and [jerky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerky "Jerky"); most of it comes from processed foods.[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-241) The [Dietary Reference Intake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dietary_Reference_Intake "Dietary Reference Intake") for sodium is 1.5 grams per day,[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-242) but most people in the United States consume more than 2.3 grams per day,[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-243) the minimum amount that promotes hypertension;[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-244) this in turn causes 7.6 million premature deaths worldwide.[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-245)

Potassium is the major [cation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cation "Cation") (positive ion) inside [animal cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_\(biology\) "Cell (biology)"),[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-potassium-238) while sodium is the major cation outside animal cells.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-potassium-238)[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-sodium-239) The [concentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concentration "Concentration") differences of these charged particles causes a difference in [electric potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_potential "Electric potential") between the inside and outside of cells, known as the [membrane potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Membrane_potential "Membrane potential"). The balance between potassium and sodium is maintained by [ion transporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_transporter "Ion transporter") proteins in the [cell membrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_membrane "Cell membrane").[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pmid16253415-246) The cell membrane potential created by potassium and sodium ions allows the cell to generate an [action potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_potential "Action potential")—a "spike" of electrical discharge. The ability of cells to produce electrical discharge is critical for body functions such as [neurotransmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurotransmission "Neurotransmission"), muscle contraction, and heart function.[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pmid16253415-246) Disruption of this balance may thus be fatal: for example, ingestion of large amounts of potassium compounds can lead to [hyperkalemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperkalemia "Hyperkalemia") strongly influencing the cardiovascular system.[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hyper-247)[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-248) Potassium chloride is used in the United States for [lethal injection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lethal_injection "Lethal injection") executions.[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-hyper-247)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Goi%C3%A2niaRadiationsource.gif/400px-Goi%C3%A2niaRadiationsource.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Goi%C3%A2niaRadiationsource.gif)

A wheel type radiotherapy device which has a long [collimator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collimator "Collimator") to focus the radiation into a narrow beam. The caesium-137 chloride radioactive source is the blue square, and gamma rays are represented by the beam emerging from the aperture. This was the radiation source involved in the Goiânia accident, containing about 93 grams of caesium-137 chloride.

Due to their similar atomic radii, rubidium and caesium in the body mimic potassium and are taken up similarly. Rubidium has no known biological role, but may help stimulate [metabolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metabolism "Metabolism"),[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-rubidium-249)[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-yale-250)[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jcp.sagepub.com-251) and, similarly to caesium,[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-rubidium-249)[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-caesium-252) replace potassium in the body causing [potassium deficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypokalemia "Hypokalemia").[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-rubidium-249)[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jcp.sagepub.com-251) Partial substitution is quite possible and rather non-toxic: a 70 kg person contains on average 0.36 g of rubidium, and an increase in this value by 50 to 100 times did not show negative effects in test persons.[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-253) Rats can survive up to 50% substitution of potassium by rubidium.[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jcp.sagepub.com-251)[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-254) Rubidium (and to a much lesser extent caesium) can function as temporary cures for hypokalemia; while rubidium can adequately physiologically substitute potassium in some systems, caesium is never able to do so.[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-yale-250) There is only very limited evidence in the form of deficiency symptoms for rubidium being possibly essential in goats; even if this is true, the trace amounts usually present in food are more than enough.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Gottschlich2001-255)[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-InselTurner2004-256)

Caesium compounds are rarely encountered by most people, but most caesium compounds are mildly toxic. Like rubidium, caesium tends to substitute potassium in the body, but is significantly larger and is therefore a poorer substitute.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-webelements-caesium-252) Excess caesium can lead to [hypokalemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypokalemia "Hypokalemia"), [arrhythmia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrhythmia "Arrhythmia"), and acute cardiac arrest,[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-257) but such amounts would not ordinarily be encountered in natural sources.[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pinsky-258) As such, caesium is not a major chemical environmental pollutant.[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pinsky-258) The [median lethal dose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_lethal_dose "Median lethal dose") (LD50) value for [caesium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium_chloride "Caesium chloride") in mice is 2.3 g per kilogram, which is comparable to the LD50 values of [potassium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_chloride "Potassium chloride") and [sodium chloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_chloride "Sodium chloride").[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-259) Caesium chloride has been promoted as an alternative cancer therapy,[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-260) but has been linked to the deaths of over 50 patients, on whom it was used as part of a scientifically unvalidated cancer treatment.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-261)

[Radioisotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioisotope "Radioisotope") of caesium require special precautions: the improper handling of caesium-137 [gamma ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_ray "Gamma ray") sources can lead to release of this radioisotope and radiation injuries. Perhaps the best-known case is the Goiânia accident of 1987, in which an improperly-disposed-of radiation therapy system from an abandoned clinic in the city of [Goiânia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goi%C3%A2nia "Goiânia"), Brazil, was scavenged from a junkyard, and the glowing [caesium salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium_chloride "Caesium chloride") sold to curious, uneducated buyers. This led to four deaths and serious injuries from radiation exposure. Together with [caesium-134](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesium-134 "Caesium-134"), [iodine-131](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iodine-131 "Iodine-131"), and [strontium-90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strontium-90 "Strontium-90"), caesium-137 was among the isotopes distributed by the [Chernobyl disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernobyl_disaster "Chernobyl disaster") which constitute the greatest risk to health.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-IAEA-107) Radioisotopes of francium would presumably be dangerous as well due to their high decay energy and short half-life, but none have been produced in large enough amounts to pose any serious risk.[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-andyscouse-209)

## Notes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alkali_metal\&action=edit\&section=52 "Edit section: Notes")]

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-1)** The symbols **Na** and **K** for sodium and potassium are derived from their Latin names, *natrium* and *kalium*; these are still the origins of the names for the elements in some languages, such as German and Russian.
2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-5)** *Caesium* is the spelling recommended by the [International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_of_Pure_and_Applied_Chemistry "International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry") (IUPAC).[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-2) The [American Chemical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Chemical_Society "American Chemical Society") (ACS) has used the spelling *cesium* since 1921,[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-3)[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-4) following *Webster's Third New International Dictionary*.
3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-group-numbering_7-0)** In both the old IUPAC and the [CAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_Abstracts_Service "Chemical Abstracts Service") systems for group numbering, this group is known as **group IA** (pronounced as "group one A", as the "I" is a [Roman numeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_numeral "Roman numeral")).[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fluck-6)
4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-8)** While hydrogen also has this electron configuration, it is not considered an alkali metal as it has very different behaviour owing to the lack of [valence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valence_electron "Valence electron") p-orbitals in [period 1 elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Period_1_element "Period 1 element").
5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-34)** In the 1869 version of Mendeleev's periodic table, copper and silver were placed in their own group, aligned with hydrogen and [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\) "Mercury (element)"), while gold was tentatively placed under [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") and the undiscovered [eka-aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallium "Gallium") in the [boron group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron_group "Boron group").
6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-44)** The [asterisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asterisk "Asterisk") denotes an [excited state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excited_state "Excited state").
7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-73)** The number given in [parentheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracket "Bracket") refers to the [measurement uncertainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_uncertainty "Standard uncertainty"). This uncertainty applies to the [least significant figure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Significant_figure "Significant figure")(s) of the number prior to the parenthesised value (ie. counting from rightmost digit to left). For instance, 1.00794(7) stands for 1.00794±0.00007, while 1.00794(72) stands for 1.00794±0.00072.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72)
8. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-74)** The value listed is the conventional value suitable for trade and commerce; the actual value may range from 6.938 to 6.997 depending on the isotopic composition of the sample.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomicweights2009-64)
9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-75)** The element does not have any stable [nuclides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclide "Nuclide"), and a value in brackets indicates the [mass number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_number "Mass number") of the longest-lived [isotope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotope "Isotope") of the element.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomicweights2007-63)[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-atomicweights2009-64)
10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-Fr-electronegativity_79-0)** [Linus Pauling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linus_Pauling "Linus Pauling") estimated the electronegativity of francium at 0.7 on the [Pauling scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauling_scale "Pauling scale"), the same as caesium;[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) the value for caesium has since been refined to 0.79, although there are no experimental data to allow a refinement of the value for francium.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78) Francium has a slightly higher ionisation energy than caesium,[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-andreev-76) 392.811(4) kJ/mol as opposed to 375.7041(2) kJ/mol for caesium, as would be expected from [relativistic effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_effects "Relativistic effects"), and this would imply that caesium is the less electronegative of the two.
